
UNIVERSAL ITEM MASTER
Functional & Technical Design Specification
Generic / Multi-Industry ERP Platform

Target Database Platform: Microsoft SQL Server
Document Version: 4.1 (Complete — 13 Chapters + Appendix A: HCM + Appendix B: Architecture + Appendix C: Reference Master Data)
Classification: Internal — Engineering Use
 
 
Table of Contents


 
1. Executive Summary
This document defines the complete functional and technical design of the Universal Item Master (UIM) module for the ERP platform. The Item Master is the single system of record for every good, service, raw material, sub-assembly, finished product, and non-stock item transacted anywhere in the organization. It is the foundation on which procurement, inventory, manufacturing, sales, finance, and quality processes are built — if the item data is wrong, every downstream process that touches it is wrong.
The design in this document is deliberately industry-agnostic. Rather than modeling a single vertical (e.g. pure manufacturing or pure retail), the Universal Item Master uses a classification hierarchy plus a dynamic (metadata-driven) attribute framework so that the same core schema can represent a manufactured sub-assembly, a retail SKU, a service line item, or a raw material lot without schema changes per industry. Industry- or unit-specific behavior is expressed through configuration (templates, mapping tables, and business rules) rather than through bespoke tables or code branches.
This chapter sets out why the project exists, who it serves, and how success will be measured. Subsequent chapters translate these objectives into a concrete data model (Chapters 2–5), functional mappings into each downstream domain (Chapter 6), operational concerns such as quality, traceability, and lifecycle (Chapters 7–9), integration contracts (Chapter 10), UI and process flows (Chapter 11), controls (Chapter 12), and rollout guidance (Chapter 13).
1.1 Purpose of This Document
This is a build specification, not a proposal. Every table, field, constraint, and rule described in later chapters is intended to be implemented as written, or explicitly revised through change control. Developers should be able to generate DDL, build API contracts, and construct UI screens directly from this document without needing to ask business stakeholders to re-explain intent.
•	Give developers an unambiguous, implementable schema and rule set for the Item Master domain.
•	Give business stakeholders a single reference for how item data will behave across every module it touches.
•	Give QA a validation matrix and exception catalogue to test against (Chapters 9 and 12).
•	Give data migration teams a defined target model and mapping strategy (Chapter 13).
•	Serve as the basis for change control — future schema changes are proposed as amendments to this document.
1.2 Scope
In scope for this specification:
•	Master data structures for item classification, identity, and dynamic attributes.
•	Cross-functional mapping of items into inventory, financial, procurement, sales, manufacturing, and quality domains.
•	Unit of measure and packaging hierarchy management, including conversions.
•	Multi-entity (multi-company / multi-business-unit) item visibility and localization of item data per unit.
•	Identification mechanisms: internal item codes, supplier part numbers, barcodes, and RFID.
•	Governance: approval workflow, audit trail, version control, and item lifecycle states.
•	Integration contracts (API payloads) for creating, updating, and querying items.
•	Data migration strategy from legacy item catalogs.
Out of scope for this specification:
•	Transactional documents themselves (purchase orders, sales orders, work orders) — this document defines the item entities those documents reference, not the documents.
•	Pricing and costing engines — this document defines where items connect to pricing/costing, not the pricing logic itself.
•	Warehouse execution logic (pick paths, slotting optimization) — only the item-level inventory policy attributes that feed those systems.
1.3 Intended Audience
Audience	How They Use This Document
Backend / Database Developers	Implement schema (Ch. 4–5), constraints, and business rules directly as written.
API / Integration Developers	Implement request/response contracts from Chapter 10 payload examples.
Frontend / UX Developers	Build screens from the wireframes and field-level validation rules in Chapters 11–12.
QA Engineers	Derive test cases from the validation matrix and exception catalogue (Ch. 9, 12).
Business Analysts / Product Owners	Verify that captured rules match operational intent; propose amendments.
Data Migration Team	Use the target schema and mapping strategy in Chapter 13 to plan legacy conversion.
Security / Compliance	Review the security matrix and audit trail design in Chapter 12.
 
2. Business Objectives
The objectives below are the criteria against which every design decision in this document should be evaluated. Where a design choice in a later chapter creates tension between two objectives (for example, strict data governance versus fast item onboarding), the trade-off and its resolution are called out explicitly in that chapter.
2.1 Primary Objectives
2.1.1 Single Source of Truth
Eliminate duplicate and inconsistent item definitions across departments and systems. Today, the same physical item is frequently described differently by purchasing, warehousing, and finance, leading to reconciliation effort, incorrect stock valuation, and failed integrations. The Universal Item Master is the sole authoritative source; all consuming systems and modules reference it rather than maintaining their own copies.
2.1.2 Universal, Industry-Agnostic Modeling
Support raw materials, sub-assemblies, finished goods, retail SKUs, services, and non-stock items within one schema, using classification and dynamic attributes rather than per-industry tables. This allows the platform to serve multiple business units or subsidiaries operating in different industries without forking the data model.
2.1.3 Data Quality and Governance
Enforce validation rules, mandatory attributes, and an approval workflow at the point of item creation and at every subsequent change, rather than relying on downstream cleanup. Every field that affects costing, compliance, or safety must be validated before an item becomes transactable.
2.1.4 Multi-Entity Support
Allow a single item definition to be shared, restricted, or locally overridden across multiple companies, business units, and warehouses, with clear rules for which attributes are global (defined once, inherited everywhere) and which are unit-specific (defined per business unit).
2.1.5 Full Traceability
Maintain a complete, immutable audit trail and version history for every item, sufficient to answer "what did this item's attributes look like on a given date" for financial audit, recall, and compliance purposes.
2.1.6 Extensibility Without Schema Change
New item categories, industries, or regulatory attribute requirements should be onboarded by adding attribute templates and configuration, not by altering core tables or shipping a code release.
2.1.7 Integration-Ready
Expose a well-defined API surface so that procurement, e-commerce, EDI, and third-party PIM/PLM systems can create and consume item data reliably, with predictable error handling.
2.2 Success Metrics
Objective	Metric	Target
Single Source of Truth	Number of item attributes duplicated in downstream systems post go-live	Zero duplicated attributes; downstream systems reference by ItemId
Data Quality	Percentage of new items rejected by validation on first submission	< 10% after 90 days (indicates upstream data capture is well-designed)
Governance	Average time from item creation request to approved, transactable status	< 2 business days for standard items
Traceability	Percentage of item attribute changes with a reconstructable historical value	100%
Extensibility	New item category onboarded without a code deployment	Achievable via configuration alone
Integration	API error rate for item create/update calls due to contract ambiguity	< 1%
2.3 Key Design Principles
These principles recur throughout the document and should guide implementation decisions not explicitly covered by a written rule:
•	Prefer configuration over code — attribute templates, classification hierarchies, and mapping rules should be data-driven.
•	Nothing is hard-deleted — items and their historical states are retained; deactivation and obsolescence are status changes, not deletions (see Chapter 8).
•	Every table carries a consistent audit footprint — CreatedBy, CreatedDate, ModifiedBy, ModifiedDate, IsActive, IsDeleted, RowVersion (see Chapter 4).
•	Validation is enforced as close to the database as possible, backed by application-layer rules — constraints are not merely advisory (see Chapter 12).
•	Entity-specific overrides never silently mutate global definitions — overrides are explicit, scoped rows, not edits to the global record (see Chapter 5).
Chapter cross-references above will resolve as each subsequent chapter is delivered. This draft covers Chapter 1 only; Chapter 2 (Universal Item Master Concept & ERP Master Data Architecture) is next.
 
3. Universal Item Master Concept & ERP Master Data Architecture
This chapter grounds the abstract objectives from Chapter 1 in a concrete data architecture, and illustrates it with a realistic reference dataset. The example used throughout this chapter is a fictional diversified enterprise, “WeavoGo,” operating simultaneously in garments manufacturing, textiles, ICT, construction, transportation, tea estate agriculture, and corporate services. This is deliberately not a single-industry example: the point of the Universal Item Master is that a laptop, a bag of cement, and a roll of fabric are all just “items” governed by the same five tables, differentiated only by classification and attribute configuration, not by schema.
3.1 The Core Idea
Most legacy ERPs bind item structure to a single industry assumption — a garments ERP hard-codes GSM and fabric composition into the item table; a construction ERP hard-codes grade and standard. When the business diversifies, these systems either bolt on unrelated columns (creating wide, mostly-null tables) or require a separate item master per business line (breaking the “single source of truth” objective from Chapter 1).
The Universal Item Master avoids both failure modes with two structural decisions:
•	A single, shared classification hierarchy (Category → Group → Sub-Group → Family → Item) that every item passes through, regardless of industry.
•	A dynamic attribute framework, where the fields that make a laptop different from a bag of cement are not columns on ItemMaster at all — they are rows in an ItemAttribute table, driven by an AttributeTemplate assigned per Item Family.
Rule of thumb used throughout this document: if a field applies to more than ~80% of items across all industries (e.g. ItemCode, ItemName, BaseUOM, IsActive), it is a fixed column on ItemMaster. If it applies only to a subset of item types (e.g. GSM, CPU, RAID level, cement grade), it is a dynamic attribute. Chapter 4 defines the exact schema for both.
3.2 Classification Hierarchy
Every item in the system is classified through exactly five levels. Each level is its own table (defined in Chapter 4), self-referencing only through its immediate parent, which keeps the hierarchy simple to query and simple to extend with new branches.
ItemCategory — broadest grouping (e.g. Raw Material, ICT Equipment, Construction Material, Fixed Asset, Service)
└─ ItemGroup — functional grouping within a category (e.g. Fabric, Networking, Cement)
└─ ItemSubGroup — product type within a group (e.g. Knitted Fabric, Router, OPC Cement)
├─ ItemFamily — brand / product line within a sub-group (e.g. Single Jersey, MikroTik, Bashundhara Cement)
└─ ItemMaster — the actual transactable item (e.g. “100% Cotton Single Jersey 180 GSM”)
This hierarchy is deliberately shallow (five levels) and deliberately uniform — a tea estate item and an ICT item both pass through the same five tables. Industries differ only in which category, group, sub-group, and family values exist for them, all of which are configuration data, not schema.
3.3 Reference Dataset: Business Entities
WeavoGo operates ten business units spanning five distinct business types. Business units are the organizational units — typically divisions, subsidiaries, or estates — that transact items. An item is defined once in ItemMaster and then explicitly mapped to the units that may use it (Section 3.9).
ID	Code	Business Entity	Business Type
1	GAR	Garments Division	Manufacturing
2	TXT	Textile Division	Manufacturing
3	EMB	Embroidery Division	Manufacturing
4	PRN	Printing Division	Manufacturing
5	ACC	Accessories Division	Manufacturing
6	ICT	ICT & Software Division	Information Technology
7	CON	Construction Division	Construction
8	TRN	Transportation Division	Logistics
9	TEA	Tea Estate	Agriculture
10	COR	Corporate Services	Administration
3.4 Reference Dataset: Item Categories
Item categories are the top of the classification hierarchy and typically correspond to how finance wants items grouped for balance-sheet and P&L purposes (material vs. asset vs. service).
ID	Code	Category	Nature
1	RAW	Raw Material	Material
2	SFG	Semi Finished	Material
3	FG	Finished Goods	Goods

5	SPR	Spare Parts	Material
6	ICT	ICT Equipment	Asset
7	CNS	Construction Material	Material
8	AST	Fixed Asset	Asset
9	SRV	Service	Service
10	OFF	Office Supplies	Material
Note the naming collision risk: the Construction Material category code (CNS) and the Corporate Services business unit code (COR) are visually similar but independent code spaces on independent tables — Chapter 4 specifies that category codes and business unit codes are validated against separate uniqueness constraints, not a shared code table.

Groups sit under a category and represent a functional grouping developers will recognize as “the filter a buyer picks first” when creating a new item.

3.8 Item Master — Mixed-Business Worked Example
The table below shows twenty real item records spanning four business types under a single ItemMaster table and a single ItemCode numbering scheme (Prefix-Group-Sequence). No schema change was required to move from garments raw materials to ICT assets to construction materials — only classification and attribute template selection changed. This is the practical proof of the “universal” claim in Section 3.1.

Observe the ItemCode pattern: {BusinessPrefix}-{GroupCode}-{Sequence}. Chapter 4 specifies this as a configurable, per-category numbering rule (not a hard-coded format), because a tea estate or media division onboarded later may need a different prefix scheme without a schema migration.
3.9 Dynamic Attribute Templates
Each Item Family (or, where finer control is needed, each Item Sub-Group) is assigned an AttributeTemplate. The template defines which attributes an item of that family must capture; the values themselves are stored per item in ItemAttribute. The seven templates below, drawn from the worked example above, show how differently two templates can look while using the identical underlying ItemAttribute/AttributeTemplate schema (defined in Chapter 4).

Two attributes recur across otherwise unrelated templates (“Brand,” “Warranty”). Chapter 4 defines a shared attribute dictionary so that “Brand” is one attribute definition reused by many templates, not redefined per template — this keeps cross-category reporting (e.g. “all items by Brand, regardless of category”) possible.
3.10 Business Entity Mapping
An item is not automatically visible or transactable in every business unit. Shared items (a laptop, a printer toner cartridge, an office chair) are explicitly mapped to every unit that may use them; unit-specific items (cement, steel rod, fabric) are mapped only to the units that actually deal in them. This mapping is a many-to-many table (BusinessUnitItem, defined in Chapter 4), not a set of flags on ItemMaster — flags would not scale past a handful of units.
Item	Garments	Textile	ICT	Construction	Corporate
Dell Laptop	✓	✓	✓	✓	✓
Cisco Switch	✓	✓	✓	✓	✓
Office Chair	✓	✓	✓	✓	✓
A4 Paper	✓	✓	✓	✓	✓
Cement	—	—	—	✓	—
Fabric	✓	✓	—	—	—
Steel Rod	—	—	—	✓	—
Printer Toner	✓	✓	✓	✓	✓
3.11 Additional Enterprise Scenarios to Design For
The scenarios below were surfaced while validating the model against WeavoGo's actual business mix, and are carried forward as binding design constraints on Chapter 4's schema and Chapter 9's exception catalogue.
Scenario	Recommended Design
Same Dell laptop purchased by different companies	One ItemMaster row; multiple BusinessUnitItem mappings; distinct warehouse assignments per unit.
Construction project-specific materials	Add ProjectId to the inventory transaction, not to ItemMaster — the item stays project-agnostic.
ICT assets requiring depreciation	Link ItemMaster to AssetMaster only after capitalization, rather than storing depreciation fields on ItemMaster directly.
Software licenses	Model as Service / Intangible Asset with license-count, activation-date, and expiry attributes via the dynamic attribute framework.
Rental equipment (excavators, generators)	Create as assets with rental-status and utilization-tracking attributes; no separate item type needed.
Shared corporate consumables	Single ItemMaster row with issue permissions granted across all business units via BusinessUnitItem.
Construction materials with varying dimensions	Use dynamic attributes (length, width, thickness) and support item variants where a true SKU-level split is warranted.
Items simultaneously purchased, sold, and manufactured	Maintain a single item with capability flags: CanPurchase, CanSell, CanManufacture, CanStock, CanTransfer (Chapter 4).
3.12 Chapter Summary
This chapter established that the entire multi-industry item catalogue — fabric, laptops, servers, cement, steel — is representable in five classification tables plus a dynamic attribute framework, with unit visibility handled by an explicit mapping table rather than schema branching. Chapter 4 now turns this concept into an actual entity-relationship diagram and table-by-table schema: primary keys, foreign keys, data types, constraints, and validation rules for every table introduced here.
 
4. Database Design — ERD & Core Item Master Schema
This chapter specifies the nine core tables that implement the classification hierarchy and dynamic attribute framework described in Chapter 3, ready for direct translation into SQL Server DDL. Supporting tables — Business Entity, Unit of Measure, and Packaging — are specified separately in Chapter 5 so that this chapter can stay focused on the item identity and classification engine.
Every table follows the standard audit footprint defined in Section 4.1. To keep each table's schema listing focused on its distinguishing columns, the seven audit columns are not repeated in every table's column list — assume they are present on every table in this document unless explicitly stated otherwise.
4.1 Standard Audit Columns
Applied to every table in this specification, without exception, including junction and history tables:
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
CreatedBy	INT	No	—	FK to UserAccount.UserId; user who created the row
CreatedDate	DATETIME2(3)	No	SYSUTCDATETIME()	UTC row creation timestamp
ModifiedBy	INT	Yes	NULL	FK to UserAccount.UserId; last user to modify the row
ModifiedDate	DATETIME2(3)	Yes	NULL	UTC timestamp of last modification
IsActive	BIT	No	1	Soft enable/disable flag; inactive rows are hidden from pick-lists but retained
IsDeleted	BIT	No	0	Soft-delete flag; see Chapter 8 for the deletion policy — rows are never hard-deleted
RowVersion	ROWVERSION	No	auto	SQL Server optimistic-concurrency token; prevents lost updates on concurrent edits
4.2 Entity-Relationship Overview
The classification chain (Section 4.3–4.6) is a strict, single-parent hierarchy:
ItemCategory  (PK: ItemCategoryId)
└─ ItemGroup  (PK: ItemGroupId, FK: ItemCategoryId)
└─ ItemSubGroup  (PK: ItemSubGroupId, FK: ItemGroupId)
└─ ItemFamily  (PK: ItemFamilyId, FK: ItemSubGroupId, FK: DefaultAttributeTemplateId)
└─ ItemMaster  (PK: ItemId, FK: ItemFamilyId + denormalized FKs up the chain)
The dynamic attribute engine (Section 4.7–4.9) is a separate, template-driven chain that ItemMaster plugs into:
AttributeDefinition  (PK: AttributeDefinitionId)  — the shared attribute dictionary
AttributeTemplate  (PK: AttributeTemplateId)  — a named bundle of attributes
└─ TemplateAttribute  (PK: TemplateAttributeId, FK: AttributeTemplateId, FK: AttributeDefinitionId)
ItemMaster.AttributeTemplateId ──→ AttributeTemplate  (which template this item uses)
ItemMaster ──→ ItemAttribute  (PK: ItemAttributeId, FK: ItemId, FK: AttributeDefinitionId)  — the actual values
ItemMaster stores denormalized ItemCategoryId, ItemGroupId, and ItemSubGroupId in addition to ItemFamilyId. This is a deliberate denormalization: it lets reporting and filtering queries join ItemMaster directly to any hierarchy level without traversing three intermediate tables, at the cost of a trigger-enforced consistency rule (Section 4.6, Business Rules) that keeps the denormalized values in sync with ItemFamily's actual ancestry.
4.3 Table: ItemCategory
Purpose: the top level of the classification hierarchy; corresponds to how finance groups items for balance-sheet and P&L reporting.
Primary Key: ItemCategoryId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: None — root of the hierarchy
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
ItemCategoryId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
CategoryCode	VARCHAR(10)	No	—	Short business code, e.g. RAW, ICT, CNS
CategoryName	VARCHAR(100)	No	—	Display name, e.g. Raw Material
Nature	VARCHAR(20)	No	—	One of: Material, Goods, Asset, Service
Description	VARCHAR(500)	Yes	NULL	Optional free-text explanation of scope
DisplayOrder	INT	No	0	Controls pick-list ordering in the UI
Constraints:
•	UQ_ItemCategory_Code — unique (CategoryCode)
•	CK_ItemCategory_Nature — CHECK (Nature IN ('Material','Goods','Asset','Service'))
Validation rules:
•	CategoryCode: 2–10 characters, uppercase A–Z and 0–9 only, no spaces.
•	CategoryName is required and must be unique among active rows (case-insensitive).
Business rules:
•	A category cannot be hard-deleted once any ItemGroup references it; attempting to delete sets IsActive = 0 instead and requires zero active child groups.
•	Changing Nature on an existing category with active items requires an explicit re-approval workflow step (Chapter 8) since it can affect financial mapping (Chapter 6).
Sample data:
ItemCategoryId	CategoryCode	CategoryName	Nature
1	RAW	Raw Material	Material
2	SFG	Semi Finished	Material
3	FG	Finished Goods	Goods
4	CON	Consumable	Material
5	SPR	Spare Parts	Material
6	ICT	ICT Equipment	Asset
7	CNS	Construction Material	Material
8	AST	Fixed Asset	Asset
9	SRV	Service	Service
10	OFF	Office Supplies	Material
4.4 Table: ItemGroup
Purpose: functional grouping within a category — typically the first filter a buyer selects when creating a new item.
Primary Key: ItemGroupId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: ItemCategoryId → ItemCategory.ItemCategoryId (ON DELETE NO ACTION)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
ItemGroupId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ItemCategoryId	INT	No	—	Parent category
GroupCode	VARCHAR(10)	No	—	Short code, unique within its category
GroupName	VARCHAR(100)	No	—	Display name, e.g. Fabric
Description	VARCHAR(500)	Yes	NULL	Optional scope note
DisplayOrder	INT	No	0	Pick-list ordering
Constraints:
•	UQ_ItemGroup_CategoryCode — unique (ItemCategoryId, GroupCode); the same code may be reused under a different category.
Validation rules:
•	GroupCode: 2–10 characters, uppercase A–Z and 0–9 only.
•	ItemCategoryId must reference an active category; attempting to attach a group to an inactive category is rejected at the API layer.
Business rules:
•	Same non-hard-delete and re-parenting-approval rules as ItemCategory (Section 4.3) apply.
Sample data:
ItemGroupId	ItemCategoryId (FK)	GroupCode	GroupName	Category Ref.
1	1	FAB	Fabric	RAW
2	1	TRM	Trims	RAW
3	1	CHM	Chemical	RAW
4	5	MAC	Machine Spare	SPR
5	6	NET	Networking	ICT
6	6	CMP	Computer	ICT
7	6	SEC	Security	ICT
8	7	CEM	Cement	CNS
9	7	STL	Steel	CNS
10	7	ELE	Electrical	CNS
4.5 Table: ItemSubGroup
Purpose: product type within a group — the level at which most catalogue browsing happens.
Primary Key: ItemSubGroupId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: ItemGroupId → ItemGroup.ItemGroupId (ON DELETE NO ACTION)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
ItemSubGroupId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ItemGroupId	INT	No	—	Parent group
SubGroupCode	VARCHAR(10)	No	—	Short code, unique within its group
SubGroupName	VARCHAR(100)	No	—	Display name, e.g. Knitted Fabric
Description	VARCHAR(500)	Yes	NULL	Optional scope note
DisplayOrder	INT	No	0	Pick-list ordering
Constraints: UQ_ItemSubGroup_GroupCode — unique (ItemGroupId, SubGroupCode).
Validation and business rules mirror Section 4.4, scoped one level down.
Sample data:
ItemSubGroupId	ItemGroupId (FK)	SubGroupCode	SubGroupName	Group Ref.
1	1	KNT	Knitted Fabric	FAB
2	1	WVN	Woven Fabric	FAB
3	2	ZIP	Zipper	TRM
4	2	BTN	Button	TRM
5	5	SWT	Network Switch	NET
6	5	RTR	Router	NET
7	6	LTP	Laptop	CMP
8	6	SRV	Server	CMP
9	8	OPC	OPC Cement	CEM
10	10	CAB	Electrical Cable	ELE
4.6 Table: ItemFamily
Purpose: brand or product line within a sub-group; the level at which a default AttributeTemplate is assigned so that new items inherit the correct attribute set automatically.
Primary Key: ItemFamilyId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: ItemSubGroupId → ItemSubGroup.ItemSubGroupId; DefaultAttributeTemplateId → AttributeTemplate.AttributeTemplateId (nullable)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
ItemFamilyId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ItemSubGroupId	INT	No	—	Parent sub-group
FamilyCode	VARCHAR(10)	No	—	Short code, unique within its sub-group
FamilyName	VARCHAR(100)	No	—	Display name, e.g. Single Jersey, Dell, MikroTik
DefaultAttributeTemplateId	INT	Yes	NULL	Template new items in this family should default to
DisplayOrder	INT	No	0	Pick-list ordering
Constraints: UQ_ItemFamily_SubGroupCode — unique (ItemSubGroupId, FamilyCode).
Business rules:
•	If DefaultAttributeTemplateId is NULL, item creation under this family requires the user to select a template explicitly — creation cannot proceed with no template at all (Chapter 8, Item Lifecycle).
•	Changing a family's DefaultAttributeTemplateId does not retroactively change already-created items' AttributeTemplateId; it only affects new items going forward.
Sample data:
ItemFamilyId	ItemSubGroupId (FK)	FamilyCode	FamilyName	SubGroup Ref.
1	1	SJ	Single Jersey	Knitted Fabric
2	1	RIB	Rib	Knitted Fabric
3	6	CISCO	Cisco	Router
4	6	MIK	MikroTik	Router
5	5	HP	HPE Aruba	Network Switch
6	7	DELL	Dell	Laptop
7	7	LEN	Lenovo	Laptop
8	8	HPE	HPE ProLiant	Server
9	9	BSC	Bashundhara Cement	OPC Cement
10	10	RR	RR Kabel	Electrical Cable
4.7 Table: AttributeDefinition
Purpose: the shared, category-agnostic dictionary of every attribute that can exist anywhere in the system. Defining “Brand” once here and reusing it across the Laptop, Server, Router, and Cement templates (Section 4.8–4.9) is what makes a cross-category report like “all items by Brand” possible without UNION-ing dissimilar columns.
Primary Key: AttributeDefinitionId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: None
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
AttributeDefinitionId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
AttributeCode	VARCHAR(50)	No	—	Unique machine code, e.g. BRAND, GSM
AttributeName	VARCHAR(100)	No	—	Display label, e.g. Brand
DataType	VARCHAR(20)	No	—	One of: Text, Number, Boolean, Date, Enum
UnitOfMeasure	VARCHAR(20)	Yes	NULL	Optional unit label for Number types, e.g. GB, inch, years
EnumOptions	NVARCHAR(MAX)	Yes	NULL	JSON array of allowed values; required when DataType = 'Enum'
Constraints:
•	UQ_AttributeDefinition_Code — unique (AttributeCode)
•	CK_AttributeDefinition_DataType — CHECK (DataType IN ('Text','Number','Boolean','Date','Enum'))
•	CK_AttributeDefinition_EnumOptions — CHECK (DataType <> 'Enum' OR EnumOptions IS NOT NULL)
Business rules:
•	An attribute definition's DataType cannot be changed once any ItemAttribute row references it — create a new AttributeDefinition and migrate instead (see Chapter 9, “Attribute template changes after transactions”).
Sample data (10 of the full dictionary; the complete set backing every template in Section 3.9 runs to several dozen rows):
Id	AttributeCode	AttributeName	DataType	UOM
1	BRAND	Brand	Text	—
2	MODEL	Model	Text	—
3	GSM	GSM	Number	gsm
4	COMPOSITION	Composition	Text	—
5	WIDTH	Width	Number	inch
6	WARRANTY	Warranty	Number	years
7	RAM	RAM	Number	GB
8	CPU	CPU	Text	—
9	GRADE	Grade	Text	—
10	RESOLUTION	Resolution	Text	—
4.8 Table: AttributeTemplate
Purpose: a named, reusable bundle of attributes assigned to an Item Family (Section 4.6) or directly to an item, corresponding to the seven worked examples in Section 3.9.
Primary Key: AttributeTemplateId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: None
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
AttributeTemplateId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
TemplateCode	VARCHAR(30)	No	—	Unique machine code, e.g. LAPTOP
TemplateName	VARCHAR(100)	No	—	Display name, e.g. Laptop
Description	VARCHAR(500)	Yes	NULL	Optional usage notes
Constraints: UQ_AttributeTemplate_Code — unique (TemplateCode).
Sample data:
Id	TemplateCode	TemplateName
1	FABRIC	Fabric
2	LAPTOP	Laptop
3	SERVER	Server
4	ROUTER	Router
5	SWITCH	Network Switch
6	CEMENT	Cement
7	STEELROD	Steel Rod
8	CCTV	CCTV Camera
9	GENERIC	Generic Consumable
10	OFFICEFURN	Office Furniture
4.9 Table: TemplateAttribute
Purpose: junction table defining exactly which attributes belong to a template, in what order, and whether each is mandatory. This is what the UI reads to render a dynamic item-creation form.
Primary Key: TemplateAttributeId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: AttributeTemplateId → AttributeTemplate; AttributeDefinitionId → AttributeDefinition
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
TemplateAttributeId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
AttributeTemplateId	INT	No	—	Parent template
AttributeDefinitionId	INT	No	—	Attribute included in this template
IsRequired	BIT	No	1	Whether this attribute must be filled before the item can be approved
DisplayOrder	INT	No	0	Field order on the item-creation form
DefaultValue	NVARCHAR(200)	Yes	NULL	Pre-filled value shown on the form, if any
Constraints: UQ_TemplateAttribute_TemplateDefinition — unique (AttributeTemplateId, AttributeDefinitionId).
Sample data (Laptop template, AttributeTemplateId = 2; AttributeDefinitionIds 11–15 extend the dictionary beyond the 10-row excerpt in Section 4.7):

1	2	1	Brand	Yes	1
2	2	2	Model	Yes	2

4.10 Table: ItemMaster
Purpose: the transactable item record itself — the table every other ERP module (procurement, inventory, sales, manufacturing, finance, quality) ultimately references. This is the most heavily constrained table in the schema.
Primary Key: ItemId (BIGINT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: ItemCategoryId → ItemCategory; ItemGroupId → ItemGroup; ItemSubGroupId → ItemSubGroup; ItemFamilyId → ItemFamily; AttributeTemplateId → AttributeTemplate; BaseUOMId → UOM (Chapter 5)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
ItemId	BIGINT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ItemCode	VARCHAR(30)	No	—	Business key; see Section 3.8 numbering pattern
ItemName	NVARCHAR(200)	No	—	Display name
Description	NVARCHAR(1000)	Yes	NULL	Long-form description
ItemCategoryId	INT	No	—	Denormalized ancestry — see Section 4.2 note
ItemGroupId	INT	No	—	Denormalized ancestry
ItemSubGroupId	INT	No	—	Denormalized ancestry
ItemFamilyId	INT	No	—	Direct parent
AttributeTemplateId	INT	Yes	NULL	Overrides the family's default template if set
BaseUOMId	INT	No	—	The item's stocking/costing unit of measure (Chapter 5)
CanPurchase	BIT	No	1	Item may appear on purchase orders
CanSell	BIT	No	0	Item may appear on sales orders
CanManufacture	BIT	No	0	Item may appear as a BOM output
CanStock	BIT	No	1	Item is tracked in inventory quantity
CanTransfer	BIT	No	1	Item may move between warehouses/units
IsSerialControlled	BIT	No	0	Each unit tracked by unique serial number
IsLotControlled	BIT	No	0	Units tracked by lot/batch number
ItemStatus	VARCHAR(20)	No	'Draft'	Draft, PendingApproval, Active, Inactive, Obsolete (Chapter 8)
VersionNumber	INT	No	1	Incremented on every approved revision (Chapter 8)
Constraints:
•	UQ_ItemMaster_Code — unique (ItemCode)
•	CK_ItemMaster_Status — CHECK (ItemStatus IN ('Draft','PendingApproval','Active','Inactive','Obsolete'))
•	CK_ItemMaster_StockFlags — CHECK (CanManufacture = 0 OR CanStock = 1) — a manufactured output must be stockable
Validation rules:
•	ItemCode: 3–30 characters, uppercase, hyphen-delimited; format enforced per-category via the numbering rule referenced in Section 3.8.
•	At least one of CanPurchase, CanSell, CanManufacture must be true — an item that can never be acquired or produced cannot be created.
•	ItemStatus cannot move directly from Draft to Active; it must pass through PendingApproval (Chapter 8's approval workflow).
Business rules:
•	A database trigger validates on INSERT/UPDATE that ItemCategoryId, ItemGroupId, and ItemSubGroupId are the true ancestors of ItemFamilyId, rejecting the write if they have drifted out of sync — this is the enforcement mechanism for the denormalization noted in Section 4.2.
•	If AttributeTemplateId is NULL, the effective template is ItemFamily.DefaultAttributeTemplateId; application code must resolve this at read time.
•	Renaming ItemCode after the item has any transactional history is blocked outright (see Chapter 9, “Item code renaming restrictions”) — only ItemName and Description may change post go-live.
Sample data (10 rows, one per Item Family from Section 4.6, to demonstrate full FK coverage; audit and flag columns omitted for width):
ItemId	ItemCode	ItemName	FamilyId (FK)	Status
1	MAT-FAB-000001	100% Cotton Single Jersey 180 GSM	1	Active
2	MAT-FAB-000002	95/5 Rib Fabric	2	Active
3	ICT-NET-000022	Cisco ISR4331 Router	3	Active
4	ICT-NET-000009	MikroTik CCR2116 Router	4	Active
5	ICT-NET-000023	HPE Aruba 2930F Switch 24-Port	5	Active
6	ICT-LTP-000006	Dell Latitude 7450 Laptop	6	Active
7	ICT-LTP-000021	Lenovo ThinkPad X1 Carbon	7	PendingApproval
8	ICT-SRV-000007	HPE ProLiant DL380 Gen11 Server	8	Active
9	CON-CEM-000013	Bashundhara OPC Cement 50 KG	9	Active
10	CON-ELE-000019	RR PVC Cable 2.5 sqmm	10	Active
Rows 3, 5, and 7 (Cisco ISR4331, HPE Aruba Switch, Lenovo ThinkPad) are supplementary items added in this chapter, beyond the original 20-item worked example in Section 3.8, specifically so every one of the ten Item Families has at least one clean, FK-consistent example item.
4.11 Table: ItemAttribute
Purpose: stores the actual attribute values for a specific item, keyed against AttributeDefinition. One row per item per attribute.
Primary Key: ItemAttributeId (BIGINT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: ItemId → ItemMaster; AttributeDefinitionId → AttributeDefinition
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
ItemAttributeId	BIGINT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ItemId	BIGINT	No	—	Item this value belongs to
AttributeDefinitionId	INT	No	—	Which attribute this value is for
ValueText	NVARCHAR(500)	Yes	NULL	Populated when AttributeDefinition.DataType = 'Text' or 'Enum'
ValueNumber	DECIMAL(18,4)	Yes	NULL	Populated when DataType = 'Number'
ValueDate	DATE	Yes	NULL	Populated when DataType = 'Date'
ValueBoolean	BIT	Yes	NULL	Populated when DataType = 'Boolean'
Constraints:
•	UQ_ItemAttribute_ItemDefinition — unique (ItemId, AttributeDefinitionId)
•	CK_ItemAttribute_SingleValue — CHECK enforcing that exactly one of ValueText, ValueNumber, ValueDate, ValueBoolean is non-null, matching the referenced AttributeDefinition.DataType
Business rules:
•	An item cannot move from PendingApproval to Active status while any TemplateAttribute marked IsRequired for its effective template has no corresponding ItemAttribute row (Chapter 8, Chapter 12).
•	Historical ItemAttribute values are never overwritten in place after an item is Active — changes create a new version row per Chapter 8's version control design, not an UPDATE.
Sample data (Dell Latitude 7450, ItemId = 6):
Id	AttributeDefId (FK)	Attribute Ref.	ValueText	ValueNumber
1	1	Brand	Dell	—
2	2	Model	Latitude 7450	—
3	8	CPU	Intel Core Ultra 7	—
4	7	RAM	—	32 (GB)
5	11	SSD	—	1 (TB)
6	12	Display	14″ FHD	—
7	13	Operating System	Windows 11 Pro	—
8	6	Warranty	—	3 (Years)
9	14	Serial No.	Auto-generated	—
10	15	Asset Tag	System-generated	—
4.12 Chapter Summary
The nine tables in this chapter — five classification tables, three attribute-engine tables, and ItemMaster itself — fully implement the concept from Chapter 3 as an implementable SQL Server schema, with every FK relationship demonstrated against the WeavoGo reference dataset. Chapter 5 extends this schema outward into Business Entity, Unit of Measure, and Packaging Hierarchy — the supporting tables that make an item transactable across multiple companies and multiple units of measure.
 
5. Database Design — Supporting Tables (Business Entity, UOM, Packaging)
Chapter 4 specified the item identity and classification engine. This chapter specifies the tables that make an item transactable across a real multi-company group: the Business Entity hierarchy and its mapping to items, the Unit of Measure dictionary, and the packaging hierarchy that governs how an item is bought, stocked, and sold in different pack sizes. Throughout, the running example expands from WeavoGo's ten divisions (Chapter 3) to the fuller “ABC Group” structure — thirteen companies spanning garments, ICT, construction, logistics, agriculture, corporate services, and healthcare — specifically to stress-test the claim that new units and new industries onboard as configuration, not schema change.
5.1 Item, Asset, or Service?
Before specifying the Business Entity tables, it is worth being explicit about a distinction that recurs throughout this document: every row in ItemMaster is one of an inventory item, a consumable, a fixed asset, or a service, and the CanStock / CanManufacture flags from Section 4.10 plus an optional link to AssetMaster (Chapter 6) are what encode the difference — not a separate table per type.
Type	Example	In ItemMaster?	Stocked?	In Asset Register?
Inventory Item	Fabric Roll	Yes	Yes	No
Consumable	A4 Paper	Yes	Yes	No
Spare Part	Bearing	Yes	Yes	No
Finished Goods	T-Shirt	Yes	Yes	No
Construction Material	Cement	Yes	Yes	No
ICT Equipment	Dell Laptop	Yes	Yes, until issued	Yes, after capitalization
Fixed Asset	Generator	Yes	No, after capitalization	Yes
Service	Internet Subscription	Yes	No	No
Software License	Microsoft 365	Yes	No	Optional (intangible asset)
A service such as an internet subscription or a software license is still an ItemMaster row — CanStock = 0, CanPurchase = 1, and its dynamic attributes capture contract-specific fields (seat count, renewal date, vendor) exactly the way a physical item's attributes capture GSM or CPU. Treating services as a special case outside ItemMaster is the single most common mistake this design avoids (see Section 3.1).
5.2 Company and Business Unit
Organizational structure is modeled as two distinct, properly typed tables rather than one self-referencing table with a flag — a legal Company can never be mistaken for an operating BusinessUnit at the schema level, statutory fields (tax registration, incorporation date) live only where they are meaningful, and every downstream table that references “which part of the organization owns this” (Warehouse, GLMapping, Department, UserAccount, and others) points unambiguously at BusinessUnit.
5.2.1 Table: Company
Purpose: the legal entity layer — a registered company with its own tax and statutory identity. A diversified group may contain several Companies, each operating one or more BusinessUnits (Section 5.2.2).
Primary Key: CompanyId (INT IDENTITY)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
CompanyId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
CompanyCode	VARCHAR(15)	No	—	Short unique code, e.g. WEAVOGO, TISWL
CompanyName	VARCHAR(200)	No	—	Full legal name, e.g. That's It Sports Ware Ltd.
TaxRegistrationNo	VARCHAR(30)	Yes	NULL	VAT/GST/tax identification number
Country	VARCHAR(60)	No	—	Country of incorporation
Constraints: UQ_Company_Code — unique (CompanyCode).
Sample data — two distinct legal entities, used throughout this document as the two worked examples:
CompanyId	Code	Company Name	Country
1	WEAVOGO	WeavoGo Group Ltd.	Bangladesh
2	TISWL	That's It Sports Ware Ltd.	Bangladesh
5.2.2 Table: BusinessUnit
Purpose: the organizational units — divisions, factories, or estates — that actually purchase, stock, issue, or consume items, each belonging to exactly one Company. This table is the practical proof of Chapter 1's “multi-entity support” objective: onboarding the Dyeing Factory, the Washing Plant, or a sixth garments production unit is a single INSERT referencing an existing CompanyId, not a schema migration.
Primary Key: BusinessUnitId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: CompanyId → Company.CompanyId
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
BusinessUnitId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
CompanyId	INT	No	—	The legal company this unit operates under
UnitCode	VARCHAR(15)	No	—	Short code, unique within its company, e.g. GAR, TISWL-U1
UnitName	VARCHAR(150)	No	—	Display name, e.g. Garments Division, TISWL Unit 1
BusinessType	VARCHAR(50)	No	—	e.g. Manufacturing, Garments Production, Embroidery, Healthcare
Constraints: UQ_BusinessUnit_CompanyCode — unique (CompanyId, UnitCode).
Business rules:
•	A new business unit requires zero changes to ItemMaster, ItemCategory, or any table in Chapter 4 — it is added here, against an existing or new CompanyId, and then explicitly authorized against existing items via BusinessUnitItem (Section 5.3).
•	Deactivating a business unit (IsActive = 0) does not delete its historical BusinessUnitItem mappings or transaction history; it only prevents new mappings and new transactions.
•	BusinessType is set per unit, not per company — a single Company routinely operates units of more than one BusinessType (Section 5.2.3).
Sample data — the WeavoGo/ABC Group thirteen units from Chapter 3, all under Company 1, extended with three additional units (rows 11–13) demonstrating configuration-only onboarding, exactly as in the prior edition of this table:
ID	CompanyId	Code	Business Unit	Business Type
1	1	GAR	Garments Division	Manufacturing
2	1	TXT	Textile Division	Manufacturing
3	1	EMB	Embroidery Division	Manufacturing
4	1	PRN	Printing Division	Manufacturing
5	1	ACC	Accessories Division	Manufacturing
6	1	ICT	ICT & Software Division	Information Technology
7	1	CON	Construction Division	Construction
8	1	TRN	Transportation Division	Logistics
9	1	TEA	Tea Estate	Agriculture
10	1	COR	Corporate Services	Administration
11	1	DYE	Dyeing Factory	Manufacturing
12	1	WSH	Washing Plant	Manufacturing
13	1	HOS	Hospital	Healthcare
5.2.3 Worked Example: One Company, Multiple Production Units of Mixed Type
A second Company demonstrates a different shape than WeavoGo's: “That's It Sports Ware Ltd.” (TISWL) operates five garments production units and one embroidery unit, all under one legal registration — most of its units share a BusinessType and differ only in which physical unit they are, rather than each unit representing a distinct type as in WeavoGo's structure above.
ID	CompanyId	Code	Business Unit	Business Type
14	2	TISWL-U1	TISWL Unit 1	Garments Production
15	2	TISWL-U2	TISWL Unit 2	Garments Production
16	2	TISWL-U3	TISWL Unit 3	Garments Production
17	2	TISWL-U4	TISWL Unit 4	Garments Production
18	2	TISWL-U4B	TISWL Unit 4B	Garments Production
19	2	TISWL-EMB1	TISWL Emb 1	Embroidery
No new table or column was needed to represent either shape — WeavoGo's “one unit per business type” structure and TISWL's “five units of one type plus one of another” structure are both just rows in the same BusinessUnit table under different CompanyId values. TISWL could add a seventh unit, or an entirely unrelated business line, tomorrow as one more row; it would never need to become a second Company unless it were a genuinely separate legal registration. BusinessUnitItem (Section 5.3) authorizes items at the unit level regardless of company — a fabric item is mapped to TISWL Unit 1 through Unit 4B individually, while embroidery thread is mapped only to TISWL Emb 1; a report rolled up “by company” simply groups unit-level authorizations back up through CompanyId.
5.3 Table: BusinessUnitItem
Purpose: the explicit many-to-many authorization between an item and the business units allowed to transact it — the formal schema behind the checkmark matrix introduced in Section 3.10. An item exists exactly once in ItemMaster; this table is what makes it visible in five warehouses or invisible in the other eight.
Primary Key: BusinessUnitItemId (BIGINT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: ItemId → ItemMaster.ItemId; BusinessUnitId → BusinessUnit.BusinessUnitId
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
BusinessUnitItemId	BIGINT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ItemId	BIGINT	No	—	The item being authorized
BusinessUnitId	INT	No	—	The business unit authorized to transact it
IsAuthorized	BIT	No	1	0 revokes access without deleting transaction history
LocalItemCode	VARCHAR(30)	Yes	NULL	Optional unit-specific alias if the unit's legacy system used a different code
AuthorizedDate	DATE	No	—	Date the mapping was granted
Constraints: UQ_BusinessUnitItem_ItemUnit — unique (ItemId, BusinessUnitId).
Business rules:
•	An item with zero active BusinessUnitItem rows cannot receive inventory transactions in any unit — the API rejects the transaction with a specific “item not authorized for this unit” error (Chapter 12).
•	Physical stock quantities per unit/warehouse are not stored on this table; they live in the inventory transaction ledger (Chapter 6). This table answers “may this unit use this item at all,” not “how many does it have.”
Sample data — reconstructing Example 1 and Example 2 from the group-wide worked scenario: a Dell laptop centrally purchased by Corporate and shared across four other units, and a Cisco switch shared across five units in a completely different combination, using the same table and the same rules:
Id	ItemId (FK)	Item Ref.	BusinessUnitId (FK)	Unit Ref.	Authorized
1	6	Dell Latitude 7450	10	Corporate Services	Yes
2	6	Dell Latitude 7450	6	ICT & Software	Yes
3	6	Dell Latitude 7450	7	Construction	Yes
4	6	Dell Latitude 7450	13	Hospital	Yes
5	6	Dell Latitude 7450	1	Garments Division	Yes
6	3	Cisco ISR4331 Router	10	Corporate Services	Yes
7	3	Cisco ISR4331 Router	1	Garments Division	Yes
8	3	Cisco ISR4331 Router	2	Textile Division	Yes
9	3	Cisco ISR4331 Router	9	Tea Estate	Yes
10	3	Cisco ISR4331 Router	13	Hospital	Yes
Notice ItemId 6 (the laptop) and ItemId 3 (the router) are each authorized against a different five-unit combination, independently, from the same two tables. Neither item required a duplicate ItemMaster row, and neither unit's list required a schema change — this is the mechanism, not just the concept, behind Chapter 3's central claim.
5.4 Table: UOM
Purpose: the master dictionary of units of measure an item can be bought, stocked, or sold in.
Primary Key: UOMId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: None
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
UOMId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
UOMCode	VARCHAR(10)	No	—	Short code, e.g. PCS, KG, MTR
UOMName	VARCHAR(50)	No	—	Display name, e.g. Piece, Kilogram
UOMType	VARCHAR(20)	No	—	One of: Count, Weight, Length, Volume, Area
DecimalPrecision	TINYINT	No	0	Number of decimal places allowed for quantities in this UOM
Constraints: UQ_UOM_Code — unique (UOMCode); CK_UOM_Type — CHECK (UOMType IN ('Count','Weight','Length','Volume','Area')).
Sample data:
ID	Code	Name	Type	Decimals
1	PCS	Piece	Count	0
2	KG	Kilogram	Weight	2
3	MTR	Meter	Length	2
4	LTR	Liter	Volume	2
5	DOZ	Dozen	Count	0
6	BOX	Box	Count	0
7	CTN	Carton	Count	0
8	BAG	Bag	Weight	0
9	ROLL	Roll	Length	0
10	PLT	Pallet	Count	0
5.5 Table: UOMConversion
Purpose: fixed, item-independent conversions between units — a dozen is always 12 pieces, regardless of what is being counted. Item-specific pack sizes (how many pieces fit in one carton of a particular item) belong in ItemPackaging (Section 5.6), not here.
Primary Key: UOMConversionId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: FromUOMId → UOM; ToUOMId → UOM
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
UOMConversionId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
FromUOMId	INT	No	—	Source unit
ToUOMId	INT	No	—	Target unit
ConversionFactor	DECIMAL(18,6)	No	—	Multiply a FromUOM quantity by this to get ToUOM
Constraints: UQ_UOMConversion_Pair — unique (FromUOMId, ToUOMId); CK_UOMConversion_Factor — CHECK (ConversionFactor > 0).
Business rules: conversions between incompatible UOMType values (e.g. Weight to Length) are rejected at the application layer, not just left to whoever enters the factor.
Sample data:
Id	From	To	Factor
1	DOZ	PCS	12
2	BOX	PCS	1 (default; overridden per item in Section 5.6)
3	KG	BAG	0.02 (i.e. 1 Bag = 50 KG)
4	MTR	ROLL	0.01 (i.e. 1 Roll = 100 MTR, fabric default)
Rows 2–4 show generic defaults only. Because carton size, bag weight, and roll length genuinely vary by item (a carton of laptops is not the same count as a carton of needles), Section 5.6 gives item-level overrides precedence over this table whenever both exist.
5.6 Table: ItemPackaging
Purpose: the item-specific packaging hierarchy — how many base units make up each larger pack level for this particular item. This is what lets “5 laptops per carton” and “50 KG per cement bag” coexist without either one distorting the other.
Primary Key: ItemPackagingId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: ItemId → ItemMaster; PackagingUOMId → UOM
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
ItemPackagingId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ItemId	BIGINT	No	—	The item this packaging level applies to
PackagingLevel	TINYINT	No	—	1 = Each, 2 = Inner Pack, 3 = Outer/Carton, 4 = Pallet
PackagingUOMId	INT	No	—	Unit this level is expressed in
QtyPerParentLevel	DECIMAL(18,4)	No	—	How many of the previous level make up one of this level
IsPurchaseUOM	BIT	No	0	Whether procurement transacts in this level by default
IsSalesUOM	BIT	No	0	Whether sales transacts in this level by default
Constraints: UQ_ItemPackaging_ItemLevel — unique (ItemId, PackagingLevel); CK_ItemPackaging_Qty — CHECK (QtyPerParentLevel > 0).
Sample data (Dell Latitude 7450 sold individually but received from Dell in cartons of 5; Bashundhara Cement bagged at 50 KG with no further pallet level defined yet):
Id	Item Ref.	Level	UOM	Qty/Parent	Purchase / Sales
1	Dell Latitude 7450	1 – Each	PCS	1	Sales UOM
2	Dell Latitude 7450	3 – Carton	CTN	5	Purchase UOM
3	Bashundhara Cement	1 – Each	BAG	1	Both
5.7 The Same Separation Pattern, Applied to Supplier, Warehouse, and Price
Business Entity, UOM, and Packaging are three instances of one recurring design decision: keep anything that can vary independently of the item's identity in its own mapping table, joined back to ItemMaster by ItemId, rather than as columns on ItemMaster itself. The same pattern applies just as strictly to three more relationships that are fully specified in Chapter 6 (Procurement and Inventory Mapping) but are worth previewing here because they complete the picture of what ItemMaster deliberately does not store:
•	SupplierItem — the same laptop sourced from Dell, Computer Source, and Smart Tech at three different supplier part numbers and three different prices; none of that lives on ItemMaster.
•	WarehouseItem — the same laptop held at 20 units in the Corporate warehouse, 35 in ICT, and 10 in Construction; stock quantity is a fact about a warehouse holding an item, not a fact about the item.
•	Purchase price — never stored on ItemMaster at all; it is a fact about a specific supplier, a specific date, and a specific item, and is looked up from a price/contract table at transaction time.
A useful test when a developer is unsure whether a new field belongs on ItemMaster: “if this item were never bought, sold, or stocked by anyone, would this field still have a meaningful value?” ItemName and GSM pass that test. SupplierPrice and WarehouseStock do not — they belong in Chapter 6.
5.8 Chapter Summary
This chapter closed the gap between a single ItemMaster row and a real multi-company group: BusinessUnit and BusinessUnitItem answer “who may use this item,” UOM and UOMConversion answer “what units can it be measured in,” and ItemPackaging answers “how does it actually get packed and shipped.” The ABC Group extension in Section 5.2 — three new units added with zero schema change — is the chapter's practical proof of Chapter 1's multi-entity-support objective. Chapter 6 now moves outward again, into the functional mappings — inventory, financial, procurement, sales, and manufacturing — that finally let an item be purchased, stocked, costed, and sold.
 
6. Functional Mapping — Inventory, Financial, Procurement, Sales, Manufacturing
This chapter is where the separation principle from Chapters 1, 3, and 5 pays off: ItemMaster itself never grows a SupplierPrice column, a GLAccount column, or a BOM column. Instead, each downstream domain gets its own mapping table, joined back to ItemId, so that adding a new domain (or a new supplier, a new tax jurisdiction, a new BOM version) never touches the core tables from Chapter 4. Twelve tables are specified below, grouped into five functional areas, and closed out with a cross-reference back to the ten real-world scenarios that motivated this design.
6.1 Inventory Mapping
Two tables answer “where is this item physically held, and under what stocking rules” — deliberately separate from the transaction ledger that records how stock moves, which is out of scope per Section 1.2.
6.1.1 Table: Warehouse
Primary Key: WarehouseId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: BusinessUnitId → BusinessUnit.BusinessUnitId
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
WarehouseId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
WarehouseCode	VARCHAR(10)	No	—	Short code, unique across the group
WarehouseName	VARCHAR(100)	No	—	Display name
BusinessUnitId	INT	No	—	Owning unit (Chapter 5)
WarehouseType	VARCHAR(20)	No	—	Main, Transit, Quarantine, RawMaterial, or FinishedGoods
Address	VARCHAR(300)	Yes	NULL	Physical location
Constraints: UQ_Warehouse_Code — unique (WarehouseCode); CK_Warehouse_Type — CHECK (WarehouseType IN ('Main','Transit','Quarantine','RawMaterial','FinishedGoods')).
Sample data:
Id	Code	Warehouse Name	BusinessUnitId (FK)	Type
1	COR-WH1	Corporate Central Warehouse	10	Main
2	ICT-WH1	ICT Equipment Store	6	Main
3	CON-WH1	Construction Site Store	7	Main
4	GAR-WH1	Garments Raw Material Store	1	RawMaterial
5	GAR-WH2	Garments Finished Goods Store	1	FinishedGoods
6	HOS-WH1	Hospital Central Pharmacy Store	13	Main
7	TXT-WH1	Textile Mill Store	2	Main
8	TEA-WH1	Tea Estate Store	9	Main
9	DYE-WH1	Dyeing Chemical Store	11	Quarantine
10	TRN-WH1	Transportation Fleet Store	8	Main
6.1.2 Table: WarehouseItem
Purpose: the current, cached stock position of an item at a specific warehouse. The transaction ledger that produces this balance is out of scope for this document (Section 1.2); this table is the queryable snapshot every other module reads.
Primary Key: WarehouseItemId (BIGINT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: ItemId → ItemMaster; WarehouseId → Warehouse
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
WarehouseItemId	BIGINT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ItemId	BIGINT	No	—	The item held
WarehouseId	INT	No	—	The warehouse holding it
QuantityOnHand	DECIMAL(18,4)	No	0	Cached current physical balance, in BaseUOM
QuantityReserved	DECIMAL(18,4)	No	0	Quantity already allocated to open orders
BinLocation	VARCHAR(30)	Yes	NULL	Optional shelf/bin reference
LastCountDate	DATE	Yes	NULL	Date of the last physical stock count
Constraints: UQ_WarehouseItem_ItemWarehouse — unique (ItemId, WarehouseId).
Business rules: QuantityOnHand is only ever written by the transaction-posting process, never edited directly by a user — manual correction happens through a stock-adjustment transaction, preserving the audit trail (Chapter 8).
Sample data (Dell Latitude 7450, ItemId = 6, matching the 100-laptop worked example from Chapter 5):
Id	WarehouseId (FK)	Warehouse Ref.	Qty on Hand	Reserved
1	1	Corporate Central Warehouse	35	0
2	2	ICT Equipment Store	30	5
3	3	Construction Site Store	10	0
4	6	Hospital Central Pharmacy Store	5	0
5	4	Garments Raw Material Store	20	0
6.1.3 Table: InventoryPolicy
Purpose: reorder and stocking control settings, scoped per item per business unit — a fast-moving laptop and a slow-moving spare part need different reorder thresholds even at the same unit.
Primary Key: PolicyId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: ItemId → ItemMaster; BusinessUnitId → BusinessUnit
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
PolicyId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ItemId	BIGINT	No	—	Item this policy governs
BusinessUnitId	INT	No	—	Entity this policy applies to
ReorderLevel	DECIMAL(18,4)	No	0	Stock level that triggers a reorder suggestion
SafetyStock	DECIMAL(18,4)	No	0	Minimum buffer never to be sold below
MaxStock	DECIMAL(18,4)	Yes	NULL	Upper stocking ceiling, if enforced
ABCClass	CHAR(1)	Yes	NULL	A, B, or C — cycle-count prioritization
Constraints: UQ_InventoryPolicy_ItemUnit — unique (ItemId, BusinessUnitId); CK_InventoryPolicy_ABC — CHECK (ABCClass IN ('A','B','C') OR ABCClass IS NULL).
Sample data:
Id	Item Ref.	Unit Ref.	Reorder Lvl	Safety Stock	ABC
1	Dell Latitude 7450	ICT & Software	10	5	A
2	Bashundhara Cement	Construction	200	50	A
3	RR PVC Cable 2.5 sqmm	Construction	500	100	B
4	100% Cotton Single Jersey	Garments Division	1000	200	A
6.2 Procurement Mapping
6.2.1 Table: Supplier
Primary Key: SupplierId (INT IDENTITY)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
SupplierId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
SupplierCode	VARCHAR(15)	No	—	Unique supplier code
SupplierName	VARCHAR(150)	No	—	Legal or trading name
Country	VARCHAR(60)	No	—	Country of registration
PaymentTermsDays	INT	No	30	Standard payment terms
TaxRegistrationNo	VARCHAR(30)	Yes	NULL	VAT/GST/tax ID
Sample data:
Id	Code	Supplier Name	Country
1	DELL	Dell Technologies	United States
2	CSRC	Computer Source Ltd.	Bangladesh
3	SMTK	Smart Tech Distribution	Bangladesh
4	BSCM	Bashundhara Cement Ltd.	Bangladesh
5	RRKB	RR Kabel Ltd.	India
6.2.2 Table: SupplierItem
Purpose: which suppliers can provide a given item, at what part number, and on what terms — independent of price, which changes far more often (Section 6.2.3).
Primary Key: SupplierItemId (BIGINT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: ItemId → ItemMaster; SupplierId → Supplier; PurchaseUOMId → UOM
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
SupplierItemId	BIGINT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ItemId	BIGINT	No	—	Item being sourced
SupplierId	INT	No	—	Supplier offering it
SupplierPartNumber	VARCHAR(50)	Yes	NULL	Supplier's own code for the item
IsPreferred	BIT	No	0	Marks the default supplier when more than one is available
LeadTimeDays	INT	No	0	Typical days from PO to delivery
MinOrderQty	DECIMAL(18,4)	No	1	Supplier's minimum order quantity, in PurchaseUOM
Constraints: UQ_SupplierItem_ItemSupplier — unique (ItemId, SupplierId); business rule — at most one SupplierItem row per item may have IsPreferred = 1 (enforced by a filtered unique index).
Sample data — the same Dell Latitude sourced from three different suppliers at three different part numbers, exactly as in the worked example:
Id	Item Ref.	Supplier Ref.	Supplier Part No.	Preferred	Lead Time
1	Dell Latitude 7450	Dell Technologies	LAT7450	Yes	21
2	Dell Latitude 7450	Computer Source Ltd.	DL7450	No	7
3	Dell Latitude 7450	Smart Tech Distribution	DELL7450	No	5
4	Bashundhara Cement	Bashundhara Cement Ltd.	OPC-50KG	Yes	2
5	RR PVC Cable 2.5 sqmm	RR Kabel Ltd.	RR-PVC-2.5	Yes	14
6.2.3 Table: SupplierPrice
Purpose: time-bound purchase price per supplier-item combination. Never stored on ItemMaster or even on SupplierItem, because price changes far more frequently than the sourcing relationship itself and must retain history for audit.
Primary Key: SupplierPriceId (BIGINT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: SupplierItemId → SupplierItem
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
SupplierPriceId	BIGINT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
SupplierItemId	BIGINT	No	—	Which supplier-item combination this price applies to
UnitPrice	DECIMAL(18,4)	No	—	Price per one PurchaseUOM
Currency	CHAR(3)	No	—	ISO 4217 currency code
EffectiveFrom	DATE	No	—	First date this price applies
EffectiveTo	DATE	Yes	NULL	Last date this price applies; NULL = still current
Business rules: EffectiveFrom/EffectiveTo ranges for the same SupplierItemId must never overlap — enforced by application logic before insert, since native SQL Server lacks a range-exclusion constraint.
Sample data:
Id	Supplier-Item Ref.	Unit Price	Currency	Effective From
1	Dell Latitude 7450 — Dell	1,200.00	USD	2026-01-01
2	Dell Latitude 7450 — Computer Source	1,180.00	USD	2026-01-01
3	Dell Latitude 7450 — Smart Tech	1,225.00	USD	2026-01-01
4	Bashundhara Cement — Bashundhara	7.50	USD	2026-06-01
6.3 Sales Mapping
6.3.1 Table: CustomerItem & SalesPrice
Purpose: the sales-side mirror of Section 6.2 — which customers may buy an item, under what customer-facing code, and at what price. Both tables follow the identical shape as SupplierItem/SupplierPrice, so full column-by-column detail is not repeated; the differences are noted below.
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
CustomerItemId	BIGINT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ItemId	BIGINT	No	—	Item being sold
CustomerId	INT	No	—	FK to Customer master (not detailed in this document)
CustomerItemCode	VARCHAR(50)	Yes	NULL	The customer's own SKU/reference for this item, if they supply one
IsAuthorized	BIT	No	1	Whether this item may currently be sold to this customer
SalesPrice mirrors SupplierPrice's structure exactly (UnitPrice, Currency, EffectiveFrom/To), keyed by CustomerItemId, with one addition: CustomerId may be NULL on the price row to represent a general list price that applies before any customer-specific override is found.
Sample data (SalesPrice, T-Shirt finished good sold to two customers at two different negotiated prices, plus a general list price):
Id	Item	Customer	Unit Price	Currency
1	Cotton Jersey T-Shirt	General List Price	4.50	USD
2	Cotton Jersey T-Shirt	Retail Buyer A	4.20	USD
3	Cotton Jersey T-Shirt	Retail Buyer B	4.35	USD
6.4 Financial Mapping
6.4.1 Table: GLMapping
Purpose: routes an item's transactions to the correct General Ledger accounts. Defined at category level by default; an item-level row, if present, overrides its category's mapping.
Primary Key: GLMappingId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: ItemCategoryId → ItemCategory (nullable); ItemId → ItemMaster (nullable); BusinessUnitId → BusinessUnit
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
GLMappingId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ItemCategoryId	INT	Yes	NULL	Category-level mapping target; NULL if this is an item-level override
ItemId	BIGINT	Yes	NULL	Item-level override target; NULL if this is a category-level default
BusinessUnitId	INT	No	—	Entity this chart-of-accounts mapping belongs to
InventoryGLCode	VARCHAR(20)	No	—	Balance-sheet inventory account
COGSGLCode	VARCHAR(20)	No	—	Cost-of-goods-sold account
SalesGLCode	VARCHAR(20)	Yes	NULL	Revenue account, where applicable
PurchaseGLCode	VARCHAR(20)	No	—	Purchase/expense account
Constraints: CK_GLMapping_OneTarget — CHECK (exactly one of ItemCategoryId, ItemId is not null).
Sample data:
Id	Target	Unit Ref.	Inventory GL	COGS GL	Purchase GL
1	Category: ICT Equipment	ICT & Software	1310	5310	2110
2	Category: Construction Material	Construction	1320	5320	2120
3	Category: Raw Material	Garments Division	1300	5300	2100
4	Item: Dell Latitude 7450 (override)	Hospital	1315	5315	2115
6.4.2 Table: TaxMapping
Purpose: which tax code and rate apply to an item, by jurisdiction. Also defined category-first with optional item-level override, for the same reasons as GLMapping.
Primary Key: TaxMappingId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: ItemCategoryId → ItemCategory (nullable); ItemId → ItemMaster (nullable)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
TaxMappingId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ItemCategoryId	INT	Yes	NULL	Category-level target
ItemId	BIGINT	Yes	NULL	Item-level override target
Jurisdiction	VARCHAR(50)	No	—	Country or tax region
TaxCode	VARCHAR(20)	No	—	Internal tax code
TaxRatePercent	DECIMAL(5,2)	No	—	Applicable rate
TaxType	VARCHAR(20)	No	—	VAT, GST, SalesTax, Exempt, or WithholdingTax
Sample data:
Id	Target	Jurisdiction	Tax Code	Rate %	Type
1	Category: ICT Equipment	Bangladesh	VAT-STD	15.00	VAT
2	Category: Construction Material	Bangladesh	VAT-STD	15.00	VAT
3	Category: Service	Bangladesh	VAT-SRV	7.50	VAT
4	Item: Paracetamol (override)	Bangladesh	VAT-EXM	0.00	Exempt
6.5 Manufacturing Mapping
6.5.1 Table: BillOfMaterial
Primary Key: BOMId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: ParentItemId → ItemMaster.ItemId (must have CanManufacture = 1)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
BOMId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ParentItemId	BIGINT	No	—	The manufactured output item
BOMVersion	INT	No	1	Increments on every revised recipe
EffectiveDate	DATE	No	—	Date this version becomes usable in production
Status	VARCHAR(20)	No	'Draft'	Draft, Active, or Obsolete
Constraints: UQ_BOM_ParentVersion — unique (ParentItemId, BOMVersion); CK_BOM_Status — CHECK (Status IN ('Draft','Active','Obsolete')).
Business rules: only one BOM version per ParentItemId may be Active at a time; activating a new version automatically sets the prior Active version to Obsolete (never deleted, per Chapter 8).
Sample data (the T-Shirt BOM referenced by BOMComponent's sample data below):
BOMId	Parent Item Ref.	Version	Effective Date	Status
1	Cotton Jersey T-Shirt	1	2026-01-01	Active
6.5.2 Table: BOMComponent
Primary Key: BOMComponentId (BIGINT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: BOMId → BillOfMaterial; ComponentItemId → ItemMaster; UOMId → UOM
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
BOMComponentId	BIGINT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
BOMId	INT	No	—	Parent BOM header
ComponentItemId	BIGINT	No	—	The input material consumed
QtyPerUnit	DECIMAL(18,6)	No	—	Quantity of the component consumed per one unit of ParentItem
UOMId	INT	No	—	Unit the quantity is expressed in
ScrapPercent	DECIMAL(5,2)	No	0	Expected wastage percentage to add to the planned quantity
Business rules: ComponentItemId can never equal its BOM's ParentItemId, and a recursive check on save rejects any BOM structure that would create a circular reference (A contains B, B contains A) — see Chapter 9.
Sample data (a simplified T-Shirt BOM, BOMId = 1):
Id	Component Item	Qty per Unit	UOM	Scrap %
1	100% Cotton Single Jersey 180 GSM	0.85	MTR	3.00
2	95/5 Rib Fabric	0.10	MTR	3.00
3	YKK Nylon Zipper 8″	0	PCS	1.00
6.6 Asset Capitalization Link
6.6.1 Table: AssetMaster
Purpose: the bridge between an inventory item and the fixed-asset register, for the Forklift and Generator category of items from Section 5.1. An item is purchased and received as ordinary stock; capitalization creates the AssetMaster row and the specific serialized unit stops being tracked as sellable inventory.
Primary Key: AssetId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: ItemId → ItemMaster; BusinessUnitId → BusinessUnit
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
AssetId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ItemId	BIGINT	No	—	Originating ItemMaster row
AssetCode	VARCHAR(20)	No	—	Unique asset register tag
CapitalizationDate	DATE	No	—	Date the unit moved from inventory to asset register
AcquisitionCost	DECIMAL(18,2)	No	—	Capitalized cost
DepreciationMethod	VARCHAR(20)	No	—	StraightLine, DecliningBalance, or UnitsOfProduction
UsefulLifeMonths	INT	No	—	Depreciation period
AccumulatedDepreciation	DECIMAL(18,2)	No	0	Running depreciation total
AssetStatus	VARCHAR(20)	No	'InUse'	InUse, UnderMaintenance, or Disposed
BusinessUnitId	INT	No	—	Owning unit
Constraints: UQ_AssetMaster_Code — unique (AssetCode); CK_AssetMaster_Method — CHECK (DepreciationMethod IN ('StraightLine','DecliningBalance','UnitsOfProduction')).
Business rules: an ItemMaster row may have zero, one, or many AssetMaster rows (a bulk-purchased, non-serialized item like office chairs may capitalize several units under one blanket asset entry, while a serial-controlled item like a generator capitalizes one row per serial number); disposing an asset sets AssetStatus = 'Disposed' and is never a hard delete.
Sample data:
Id	Item Ref.	Asset Code	Acquisition Cost	Method	Status
1	Toyota Forklift 3 Ton	AST-000001	28,500.00	StraightLine	InUse
2	Diesel Generator 100 KVA	AST-000002	15,200.00	StraightLine	InUse
3	HPE ProLiant DL380 Gen11 Server	AST-000003	9,800.00	DecliningBalance	InUse
6.7 Project-Based Consumption (Illustrative)
Construction materials issued to a specific project are a transaction concern, not a master-data concern — consistent with Section 1.2's exclusion of transactional documents from this specification. ItemMaster and WarehouseItem stay project-agnostic; only the material-issue transaction records which project consumed the stock. The shape of that transaction (not a formal schema, since transactional documents are out of scope here) looks like:
Field	Example Value
Transaction Type	Project Material Issue
ProjectId	ABC Factory Building Extension
ItemId	Bashundhara OPC Cement 50 KG
QuantityIssued	500 Bags
WarehouseId	Construction Site Store
IssueDate	2026-07-15
6.8 Scenario Cross-Reference
The ten real-world examples that motivated this chapter, and exactly which tables now account for each one:
Scenario	Tables Involved	Chapter
Dell laptop shared across 5 units	ItemMaster, BusinessUnitItem, WarehouseItem	Ch. 4, 5, 6
Cisco switch shared across 5 units	ItemMaster, BusinessUnitItem	Ch. 4, 5
A4 Paper used everywhere	ItemMaster, BusinessUnitItem, WarehouseItem	Ch. 4, 5, 6
Cement bought by Construction and Corporate	ItemMaster, WarehouseItem, InventoryPolicy	Ch. 4, 6
Steel Rod used across projects	ItemMaster, WarehouseItem, (Project Issue transaction)	Ch. 4, 6
Internet Bandwidth (service)	ItemMaster (CanStock=0), SupplierItem, GLMapping, TaxMapping	Ch. 4, 6
Microsoft 365 License	ItemMaster (CanStock=0), ItemAttribute (license attrs)	Ch. 4
Generator Diesel	ItemMaster, SupplierItem, WarehouseItem	Ch. 4, 6
Forklift (asset)	ItemMaster, AssetMaster	Ch. 4, 6
Sewing Machine Needle	ItemMaster, BusinessUnitItem, WarehouseItem	Ch. 4, 5, 6
6.9 Chapter Summary
Twelve tables across five functional areas — Warehouse/WarehouseItem/InventoryPolicy, Supplier/SupplierItem/SupplierPrice, CustomerItem/SalesPrice, GLMapping/TaxMapping, and BillOfMaterial/BOMComponent, plus the AssetMaster bridge — complete the functional footprint of the Universal Item Master. Every one of them joins back to ItemMaster by ItemId and adds nothing to ItemMaster itself, which is the design discipline this entire document has been building toward since Chapter 1. Chapter 7 turns to the operational concerns that apply on top of this foundation: quality control parameters, barcode and RFID identification, and document attachment.
 
7. Quality Control Parameters, Barcode & RFID, Document Attachment
This chapter specifies the tables that make an item traceable and verifiable after it has been classified (Chapter 4), mapped to the units that use it (Chapter 5), and connected to procurement, finance, and manufacturing (Chapter 6). It closes with a worked example — a hospital simultaneously managing a batch-and-expiry-controlled medicine and a serial-and-calibration-controlled MRI machine — through the exact same eight tables.
7.1 Quality Control Parameters
Quality specifications follow the same template pattern as dynamic attributes (Section 4.7–4.9): a shared template assigned to an item family, with a critical difference — a QC parameter carries a tolerance range and a test method, because the point of a QC parameter is to pass or fail a measured value, not just to describe the item.
7.1.1 Table: QCParameterTemplate
Primary Key: QCTemplateId (INT IDENTITY)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
QCTemplateId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
TemplateCode	VARCHAR(30)	No	—	Unique machine code, e.g. CEMENT-QC
TemplateName	VARCHAR(100)	No	—	Display name, e.g. Cement Quality Test
Description	VARCHAR(500)	Yes	NULL	Scope notes
Constraints: UQ_QCParameterTemplate_Code — unique (TemplateCode).
Sample data:
Id	TemplateCode	TemplateName
1	CEMENT-QC	Cement Quality Test
2	FABRIC-QC	Fabric Quality Test
3	MEDICINE-QC	Pharmaceutical Batch Release Test
4	STEEL-QC	Steel Rod Mechanical Test
5	ELECTRICAL-QC	Electrical Cable Safety Test
6	ICT-QC	ICT Equipment Goods-In Inspection
7.1.2 Table: QCParameter
Purpose: the individual measurable specifications within a template, each with an acceptable range and a defined test method.
Primary Key: QCParameterId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: QCTemplateId → QCParameterTemplate
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
QCParameterId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
QCTemplateId	INT	No	—	Parent QC template
ParameterName	VARCHAR(100)	No	—	e.g. Compressive Strength, GSM Tolerance
DataType	VARCHAR(20)	No	—	Number, Text, or Boolean
MinValue	DECIMAL(18,4)	Yes	NULL	Lower acceptable bound, for Number parameters
MaxValue	DECIMAL(18,4)	Yes	NULL	Upper acceptable bound, for Number parameters
UnitOfMeasure	VARCHAR(20)	Yes	NULL	e.g. MPa, %, mg
TestMethod	VARCHAR(150)	Yes	NULL	Referenced standard, e.g. ASTM C150, BSTI
IsCritical	BIT	No	1	A failed critical parameter blocks the whole lot; a non-critical failure only flags for review
Sample data:
Id	Template Ref.	Parameter	Range	UOM	Critical
1	Cement Quality Test	Compressive Strength (28-day)	42.5 – 52.5	MPa	Yes
2	Cement Quality Test	Initial Setting Time	45 – 375	minutes	Yes
3	Fabric Quality Test	GSM Tolerance	176 – 184	gsm	Yes
4	Fabric Quality Test	Shrinkage	0 – 3	%	No
5	Pharmaceutical Batch Release Test	Assay (Active Ingredient)	95 – 105	%	Yes
6	Pharmaceutical Batch Release Test	Moisture Content	0 – 5	%	Yes
7	Steel Rod Mechanical Test	Yield Strength	min 60	ksi	Yes
8	Electrical Cable Safety Test	Insulation Resistance	min 100	MΩ	Yes
7.1.3 Table: ItemQCProfile
Purpose: assigns a QC template to an item family (default) or an individual item (override), mirroring the GLMapping/TaxMapping override pattern from Chapter 6.
Primary Key: ItemQCProfileId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: ItemFamilyId → ItemFamily (nullable); ItemId → ItemMaster (nullable); QCTemplateId → QCParameterTemplate
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
ItemQCProfileId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ItemFamilyId	INT	Yes	NULL	Family-level target
ItemId	BIGINT	Yes	NULL	Item-level override target
QCTemplateId	INT	No	—	Which QC template applies
IsMandatory	BIT	No	1	Whether goods-in is blocked without a passed inspection
InspectionFrequency	VARCHAR(20)	No	'EveryBatch'	EveryBatch, Sampling, or Annual
Constraints: CK_ItemQCProfile_OneTarget — CHECK (exactly one of ItemFamilyId, ItemId is not null).
Sample data:
Id	Target	QC Template	Mandatory	Frequency
1	Family: Bashundhara Cement	Cement Quality Test	Yes	EveryBatch
2	Family: Single Jersey	Fabric Quality Test	Yes	Sampling
3	Item: Paracetamol 500mg	Pharmaceutical Batch Release Test	Yes	EveryBatch
7.2 Batch, Lot, and Serial Tracking
Two tables implement the IsLotControlled and IsSerialControlled flags defined on ItemMaster in Section 4.10 — one per traceability model, since a bag of cement and an MRI machine are tracked completely differently.
7.2.1 Table: ItemLot
Primary Key: ItemLotId (BIGINT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: ItemId → ItemMaster; WarehouseId → Warehouse
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
ItemLotId	BIGINT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ItemId	BIGINT	No	—	Must reference an item with IsLotControlled = 1
LotNumber	VARCHAR(30)	No	—	Manufacturer or internally assigned lot/batch number
ManufactureDate	DATE	Yes	NULL	Date of manufacture
ExpiryDate	DATE	Yes	NULL	Date after which the lot may not be issued or sold
WarehouseId	INT	No	—	Current holding warehouse
QuantityReceived	DECIMAL(18,4)	No	—	Original received quantity for this lot
QCStatus	VARCHAR(20)	No	'Pending'	Pending, Passed, Failed, or Quarantined
Constraints: UQ_ItemLot_ItemLotNumber — unique (ItemId, LotNumber); CK_ItemLot_QCStatus — CHECK (QCStatus IN ('Pending','Passed','Failed','Quarantined')).
Business rules:
•	Stock cannot be issued from a lot whose QCStatus is Pending, Failed, or Quarantined — enforced at the transaction layer, not just the UI.
•	If ItemQCProfile marks the item's family as mandatory for QC (Section 7.1.3), QCStatus cannot move to Passed without at least one recorded pass against every IsCritical QCParameter.
•	An item whose AttributeTemplate includes an expiry-relevant attribute (e.g. the Cement template's Expiry field, Section 3.9.5) should have ExpiryDate populated on every lot; goods-in validation warns, but does not hard-block, if it is left NULL.
Sample data:
Id	Item Ref.	Lot Number	Expiry Date	Warehouse	QC Status
1	Bashundhara OPC Cement 50 KG	BSC-2026-0715	2026-10-13	Construction Site Store	Passed
2	Paracetamol 500mg	PARA-2026-A12	2028-03-31	Hospital Central Pharmacy Store	Passed
3	Paracetamol 500mg	PARA-2026-A13	2028-04-30	Hospital Central Pharmacy Store	Pending
4	100% Cotton Single Jersey 180 GSM	SJ-2026-0601	—	Garments Raw Material Store	Passed
7.2.2 Table: ItemSerial
Purpose: unit-level tracking for serialized items — individual laptops, generators, or medical equipment where each physical unit needs its own history, not just a lot.
Primary Key: ItemSerialId (BIGINT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: ItemId → ItemMaster; WarehouseId → Warehouse (nullable); LinkedAssetId → AssetMaster (nullable)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
ItemSerialId	BIGINT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ItemId	BIGINT	No	—	Must reference an item with IsSerialControlled = 1
SerialNumber	VARCHAR(50)	No	—	Manufacturer serial number
WarehouseId	INT	Yes	NULL	Current warehouse; NULL once issued or capitalized
Status	VARCHAR(20)	No	'InStock'	InStock, Issued, UnderRepair, or Retired
WarrantyExpiryDate	DATE	Yes	NULL	End of manufacturer warranty
LastCalibrationDate	DATE	Yes	NULL	For equipment requiring periodic calibration
NextCalibrationDueDate	DATE	Yes	NULL	Drives calibration-due alerts
LinkedAssetId	INT	Yes	NULL	Set once this unit is capitalized (Section 6.6)
Constraints: UQ_ItemSerial_ItemSerialNumber — unique (ItemId, SerialNumber).
Business rules: a unit with a non-NULL NextCalibrationDueDate that has passed is automatically flagged and, depending on unit policy, blocked from active use until recalibrated — relevant to regulated equipment such as the Hospital MRI example in Section 7.4.
Sample data:
Id	Item Ref.	Serial No.	Status	Next Calibration	Linked Asset
1	HPE ProLiant DL380 Gen11 Server	SN-HPE-88213	InStock	—	AST-000003
2	Siemens MRI Scanner 1.5T	SN-MRI-04471	Issued	2026-11-01	AST-000004
3	Toyota Forklift 3 Ton	SN-FLT-22190	Issued	—	AST-000001
7.3 Barcode & RFID
7.3.1 Table: ItemBarcode
Purpose: printable/scannable identifiers, one row per packaging level (Section 5.6) — an Each-level barcode differs from its Carton-level barcode for the same item.
Primary Key: ItemBarcodeId (BIGINT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: ItemId → ItemMaster; ItemPackagingId → ItemPackaging (nullable)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
ItemBarcodeId	BIGINT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ItemId	BIGINT	No	—	Item this barcode identifies
ItemPackagingId	INT	Yes	NULL	Which pack level this barcode represents; NULL = Each
BarcodeType	VARCHAR(20)	No	—	EAN13, UPC-A, Code128, or QR
BarcodeValue	VARCHAR(50)	No	—	The scannable value
IsPrimary	BIT	No	1	The default barcode printed/scanned when more than one exists at the same level
Constraints: UQ_ItemBarcode_Value — unique (BarcodeValue) — a barcode value must be globally unique, never reused across items.
Sample data:
Id	Item Ref.	Pack Level	Type	Barcode Value
