A.10 Separation & Final Settlement
The closing half of “hire to settlement.” An employee's departure is never a DELETE, or even a simple status flip — it is a recorded event with a defined clearance and payout process, the people-side equivalent of Chapter 8's ItemObsolescence.
A.10.1 Table: SeparationType
Primary Key: SeparationTypeId (INT IDENTITY)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
SeparationTypeId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
TypeName	VARCHAR(30)	No	—	Resignation, Termination, Retirement, EndOfContract, or Deceased
Sample data:
Id	Type Name
1	Resignation
2	Termination
3	Retirement
4	EndOfContract
5	Deceased
A.10.2 Table: EmployeeSeparation
Purpose: the separation event itself — required before EmployeeStatus (Section A.3.2) may move to Separated, the same pairing Section 8.5 requires between ItemMaster.ItemStatus = Obsolete and an ItemObsolescence row.
Primary Key: SeparationId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: EmployeeId → Employee; SeparationTypeId → SeparationType
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
SeparationId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
EmployeeId	INT	No	—	The employment ending
SeparationTypeId	INT	No	—	How it ended
NoticeDate	DATE	No	—	Date notice was given
LastWorkingDate	DATE	No	—	Final working day
Reason	VARCHAR(500)	Yes	NULL	Free-text reason
ClearanceStatus	VARCHAR(20)	No	'Pending'	Pending, InProgress, or Cleared
Business rules: EmployeeStatus cannot move to Separated until ClearanceStatus = 'Cleared' — which itself requires every EmployeeClearanceItem (Section A.10.3) to be closed.
Sample data — Jahangir Alam's first employment stint (EmployeeId 107) ending in resignation:
Id	Employee Ref.	Type Ref.	Notice Date	Last Working Date	Clearance Status
1	Jahangir Alam (Employee 107)	Resignation	2024-08-01	2024-08-31	Cleared
A.10.3 Table: EmployeeClearanceItem
Purpose: department-wise exit clearance, including asset return — the point where HCM and the Item Master genuinely meet: an ICT engineer's laptop clearance resolves against ItemSerial (Section 7.2.2), the same serialized unit tracked since Chapter 6's asset capitalization.
Foreign Keys: SeparationId → EmployeeSeparation; ClearingDepartmentId → Department; ItemSerialId → ItemSerial (nullable, for asset-return line items)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
EmployeeClearanceItemId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
SeparationId	INT	No	—	Parent separation event
ClearingDepartmentId	INT	No	—	Department responsible for this clearance line, e.g. IT, Finance, Stores
ItemSerialId	INT	Yes	NULL	Specific asset being returned, if this line is an asset return
ClearanceItemStatus	VARCHAR(20)	No	'Pending'	Pending, Cleared, or Waived
A departing network engineer's laptop clearance line references the exact ItemSerialId issued to them back in Section 7.2.2 — on Cleared, that unit's ItemSerial.Status (Section 7.2.2) reverts to InStock and it re-enters the available pool, closing the loop between the two masters this document specifies without either one needing to know about the other's internal schema.
Sample data (Separation 1 — Jahangir Alam):
Id	Clearing Department	Asset Ref. (ItemSerial)	Status
1	Site Procurement	— (no asset issued)	Cleared
2	Finance & Accounts	— (advance fully repaid)	Cleared
A.10.4 Table: FinalSettlementComponent
Purpose: the settlement-side counterpart to PayComponent — named, auditable line items rather than one lump “final payment” figure.
Primary Key: FinalSettlementComponentId (INT IDENTITY)
When ComponentCode = LEAVE_ENCASH, the days encashed at settlement are capped at LeavePolicy.MaxEncashableDays (Section A.7.11) for the departing employee's leave type, employment type, and nationality category — the identical ceiling Section A.7.13 applies to in-service encashment, so final settlement cannot be used to pay out more days than the employee could ever have received while still employed.
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
FinalSettlementComponentId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ComponentCode	VARCHAR(30)	No	—	Unique code, e.g. UNPAID_SALARY, LEAVE_ENCASH, GRATUITY, PF_PAYOUT, NOTICE_PAY, RECOVERY
ComponentName	VARCHAR(100)	No	—	Display name
ComponentType	VARCHAR(20)	No	—	Payable or Recovery
Sample data:
Id	Code	Component	Type
1	UNPAID_SALARY	Unpaid Salary to Last Working Date	Payable
2	LEAVE_ENCASH	Earned Leave Encashment	Payable
3	GRATUITY	Gratuity	Payable
4	PF_PAYOUT	Provident Fund Payout	Payable
5	NOTICE_PAY	Notice Period Pay (in lieu)	Payable
6	ADVANCE_RECOVERY	Outstanding Advance Recovery	Recovery
7	ASSET_RECOVERY	Unreturned Asset Value Recovery	Recovery
A.10.5 Table: EmployeeFinalSettlement
Purpose: the header record totaling FinalSettlementComponent lines into one payable amount — the closing record of the entire hire-to-settlement lifecycle this appendix models.
Primary Key: FinalSettlementId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: SeparationId → EmployeeSeparation
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
FinalSettlementId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
SeparationId	INT	No	—	The separation this settlement closes out
TotalPayable	DECIMAL(18,2)	No	0	Sum of Payable-type component lines
TotalRecovery	DECIMAL(18,2)	No	0	Sum of Recovery-type component lines
NetSettlementAmount	DECIMAL(18,2)	No	0	TotalPayable minus TotalRecovery
SettlementStatus	VARCHAR(20)	No	'Draft'	Draft, Approved, Paid
SettlementDate	DATE	Yes	NULL	Date of actual payment
Constraints: CK_FinalSettlement_Net — CHECK (NetSettlementAmount = TotalPayable − TotalRecovery).
Business rules: SettlementStatus follows Draft → Approved → Paid through the same ApprovalWorkflowTemplate mechanism as every other approval gate in this document (Section 8.2), typically requiring Finance Controller sign-off given the direct GL impact.
Sample data — Jahangir Alam's settlement (SeparationId 1), itemized against FinalSettlementComponent (Section A.10.4): Unpaid Salary 12,400 + Leave Encashment 6,200 + Gratuity 28,000 = TotalPayable 46,600; ADVANCE_RECOVERY 3,500 = TotalRecovery 3,500:
Id	Separation Ref.	Total Payable	Total Recovery	Net Amount	Status
1	Jahangir Alam	46,600	3,500	43,100	Paid
A.11 Worked Example: Full Lifecycle, One Employee
Jahangir Alam's two employment stints (Section A.3.2, EmployeeId 107 and 108) traced through every table in this appendix:
•	JobRequisition raised for Site Supervisor, Construction Division; approved through the standard workflow (Section A.4.1).
•	Candidate record created on application; CandidateApplication reaches Stage = Hired; Person row created; Employee row 107 created with EmploymentSequenceNumber = 1, EmployeeStatus = Probation.
•	EmployeeOnboarding tasks completed; ConfirmationDate set; EmployeeStatus moves to Active.
•	EmployeeCompensation assigns BASIC and CONVEYANCE components; EmployeeBenefitEnrollment adds him to the Provident Fund plan; EmployeeLeaveBalance tracks his Earned Leave accrual.
•	Eighteen months in, EmployeeSeparation is raised with SeparationTypeId = Resignation; EmployeeClearanceItem lines are opened for IT (laptop return, resolving against his issued ItemSerialId) and Finance; once all lines are Cleared, ClearanceStatus = Cleared and EmployeeStatus moves to Separated (row 107).
•	EmployeeFinalSettlement totals UNPAID_SALARY, LEAVE_ENCASH, and GRATUITY against ADVANCE_RECOVERY, reaching a NetSettlementAmount; SettlementStatus reaches Paid.
•	Eighteen months later, a new JobRequisition for the same Designation is filled by the same Person — Employee row 108 is created with EmploymentSequenceNumber = 2, a new EmployeeCode, and a fresh Probation → Active cycle, while row 107 and everything under it (compensation history, leave history, the settlement itself) remains exactly as it was.
No table in this appendix was touched twice for two different purposes, and nothing was deleted at any step — the same two disciplines that have held for the Item Master since Chapter 4 hold for the Employee Master across an entire hire-to-settlement lifecycle, including a rehire.
A.12 System Access Layer
Role, UserAccount, and UserRole answer a narrower question than Employee does: not “who works here,” but “who can log in, and with what permissions.”
A.12.1 Table: Role
Distinct from Designation (Section A.2.3): Designation is an HR job title; Role is a system permission grant.
Primary Key: RoleId (INT IDENTITY)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
RoleId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
RoleCode	VARCHAR(30)	No	—	Unique machine code
RoleName	VARCHAR(100)	No	—	Display name
Sample data (matching Chapter 12's security matrix exactly):
Id	RoleCode	RoleName
1	CATEGORY_MGR	Category Manager
2	FINANCE_CTRL	Finance Controller
3	QC_MANAGER	QC Manager
4	IT_SECURITY	IT Security Review
5	WAREHOUSE_CLERK	Warehouse Clerk
6	SALES_USER	Sales User
7	READ_ONLY	Read-Only Viewer
8	SYSTEM_ADMIN	System Administrator
A.12.2 Table: UserAccount
Purpose: the FK target every CreatedBy/ModifiedBy column in this document has pointed to since Section 4.1 — a thin login record referencing Employee, never duplicating a person's name a second time.
Primary Key: UserAccountId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: EmployeeId → Employee.EmployeeId
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
UserAccountId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key — stored in every CreatedBy/ModifiedBy column
EmployeeId	INT	No	—	The employment this login belongs to
Email	VARCHAR(150)	No	—	Login/notification address
IsActive	BIT	No	1	Disabling access without deleting the Employee record
Constraints: UQ_UserAccount_Email — unique (Email); UQ_UserAccount_Employee — unique (EmployeeId).
Business rules: on separation, UserAccount.IsActive is set to 0 in the same transaction as EmployeeClearanceItem's IT-department line clearing — access revocation is part of clearance, not a separate manual step someone can forget.
Sample data:
Id	Employee Ref.	Email	Active
1	Farhan Ahmed (EmployeeId 101)	farhan.ahmed@weavogo.com	Yes
2	Nusrat Jahan (EmployeeId 102)	nusrat.jahan@weavogo.com	Yes
3	Karim Hossain (EmployeeId 103)	karim.hossain@weavogo.com	Yes
A.12.3 Table: UserRole
Foreign Keys: UserAccountId → UserAccount; RoleId → Role; BusinessUnitId → BusinessUnit
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
UserRoleId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
UserAccountId	INT	No	—	The user granted the role
RoleId	INT	No	—	The role granted
BusinessUnitId	INT	No	—	The unit this grant is scoped to
Constraints: UQ_UserRole_UserRoleUnit — unique (UserAccountId, RoleId, BusinessUnitId).
Business rules: unchanged from the earlier version of this appendix — an ApprovalStep's ApproverRole check (Section 8.2, Section A.14) resolves against this table exactly as before; only the identity chain beneath UserAccount got deeper.
Sample data:
Id	User Ref.	Role Ref.	Unit Ref.
1	Farhan Ahmed	Category Manager	ICT & Software Division
2	Nusrat Jahan	IT Security Review	ICT & Software Division
3	Karim Hossain	Finance Controller	Corporate Services
A.13 Amendments to Earlier Chapters
•	Every audit column described since Section 4.1 as “FK to UserAccount” now resolves through UserAccount → Employee → Person — a full identity chain rather than a single flat row.
•	ApprovalStep.ApproverRole (Section 8.2.2) remains an INT FOREIGN KEY to Role.RoleId (Section A.12.1), unchanged by this appendix's rebuild.
•	Chapter 6.6.1's AssetMaster and Chapter 7.2.2's ItemSerial now have a formal consumer on the HR side: EmployeeClearanceItem (Section A.10.3) is the first table in this document to reference ItemSerialId from outside the Item Master's own chapters, closing the loop between an issued asset and the employee it was issued to.
•	Section A.7 (Time, Attendance & Leave Policy) was substantially expanded: the original flat ShiftMaster/EmployeeShiftAssignment pair is replaced by a roster engine (ShiftRoster, ShiftRosterPattern, EmployeeShiftRosterAssignment) supporting both fixed weekly patterns and rotating multi-shift cycles, plus ShiftAllowance and SpecialPeriod/SpecialPeriodAllowance for shift-linked and calendar-bound pay (Tiffin Bill, Iftar Bill), and OvertimePolicy for day-type-aware overtime multipliers — none of which existed in the earlier version of this appendix.
•	Section A.7's roster engine was further extended for dynamism: ShiftRosterPattern is now effective-dated so a factory's standard pattern can change over time without editing history in place; a new EmployeeShiftOverride table handles ad hoc call-ins, swaps, and coverage on a single date without touching the underlying roster; and ShiftAllowance gained an optional BusinessUnitId override, following the same default-plus-override lookup already used by GLMapping and TaxMapping (Section 6.4). Separately, LeaveType gained an AccrualBasis (CalendarYear or ServiceYear) and EmployeeLeaveBalance now tracks an explicit LeaveYearStartDate/EndDate window rather than a bare calendar Year, so Earned Leave can be anchored to an employee's Employee.JoiningDate anniversary instead of the fiscal calendar.
•	Section A.7.1 gained ShiftSegment (for split/changeover shifts with a built-in unpaid gap), ShiftGroup and ShiftGroupMember (named, ordered shift sets, e.g. a Two-Shift Set and a Three-Shift Set in concurrent use across the group). ShiftRoster gained ShiftGroupId, WeeksPerShift, WeeklyHolidayDayOfWeek, and TransitionShiftId, letting a Rotating roster resolve automatically week by week with zero manually-entered ShiftRosterPattern rows for the common case — including automatically substituting a changeover shift on the single day a rotation advances from one shift to the next.
•	LeavePolicy gained MaxCarryForwardDays and IsEncashable; EmployeeLeaveBalance was rebuilt from a single DaysAvailable figure into a full ledger (OpeningBalance, AccruedDays, ConsumedDays, EncashedDays), so a carried-forward Earned Leave balance is traceable back to the service-year window it came from; and a new LeaveEncashment table (Section A.7.13) pays out unused days above the carry-forward cap while an employee is still in service, kept deliberately distinct from FinalSettlementComponent's separation-time encashment (Section A.10.4).
•	Three further refinements: Section A.7.7 gained SpecialPeriodShiftOverride, so shift clock times — not just allowances — can be temporarily replaced during a SpecialPeriod like Ramadan, shift by shift; Person (Section A.3.1) gained Nationality, and LeavePolicy gained a NationalityCategory scope alongside EmploymentType, so a foreign national's 30-day Earned Leave entitlement — and independently, whether it carries forward at all — is ordinary policy configuration rather than a special case; and LeavePolicy gained MaxEncashableDays, a hard ceiling applied identically by LeaveEncashment (Section A.7.13) and FinalSettlementComponent (Section A.10.4), so the same employee cannot be paid a different amount for the same unused leave depending on whether it is encashed in-service or at separation.
A.14 Appendix Summary
This appendix replaces the earlier flat Employee table with a proper HCM foundation: Person and Employee separated so that rehires never lose history; a three-level Job Architecture sized to how job titles actually vary, not forced into Item's five levels; a dynamic attribute engine identical in shape to the Item Master's; and five functional areas — recruitment, compensation, benefits, time/leave, performance — built the same way Chapter 6 built the Item Master's functional mappings, each a separate table joined back to Employee rather than a column bolted onto it. Separation and final settlement close the lifecycle exactly the way Chapter 8 closes an item's: a governed status transition, a reasoned record of why, and a computed, itemized outcome, never a bare DELETE or a silent status flip. The same three disciplines — classification over hard-coded columns, dynamic attributes over industry-specific fields, and mapping tables over duplicated data — now run all the way from a bag of cement to a person's last day of employment. The shift model added in Section A.7 follows the same pattern once more: a rotating roster is just a repeating cycle looked up by modular arithmetic, a shift-linked allowance is a rate joined to a shift rather than duplicated per employee, and a Ramadan-only Iftar Bill is a bounded calendar period rather than a hard-coded date range — configuration over code, extended one more time, all the way down to what an employee is paid for working a Friday night shift during Ramadan.
 
Appendix B: System Architecture Design
Chapters 1–13 and Appendix A specify what this platform's data looks like — the Item Master, the organizational foundation, the HCM module. This appendix specifies how a system built on that data model is actually structured, deployed, secured, and operated: the logical and physical architecture, service boundaries, integration patterns, multi-tenancy model, and the operational concerns (security, scalability, observability, deployment) that apply across every module already specified.
As with Appendix A, this is deliberately an appendix rather than a chapter renumbering the rest of the document: the data model in Chapters 4–13 and Appendix A is what every one of the .NET microservices described below persists — the schema does not change depending on how many services it is split across. This appendix describes the platform's committed architecture: ASP.NET Core microservices on .NET, decomposed along the service boundaries in Section B.3, not one option among several.
B.1 Architecture Vision & Guiding Principles
The same three disciplines that have run through this entire document — classification over hard-coded columns, dynamic attributes over industry-specific fields, mapping tables over duplicated data — extend naturally into six architectural principles:
•	API-first: every capability is exposed through the versioned REST contract established in Chapter 10 before any UI consumes it; the UI is a client of the API, never a shortcut around it.
•	Domain-aligned service boundaries: services are cut along the same bounded contexts this document already uses — Item Master, Organization & Identity, HCM — not along technical layers, so a service's schema maps directly back to a chapter or appendix section.
•	Single responsibility per service: each service in Section B.3 owns exactly one bounded context's data and rules — WeavoMM never reaches into WeavoWM's tables, and neither reaches into the other's business logic, even though procurement and warehousing are tightly related and sit right next to each other in the Business Services tier. A service that starts accumulating a second, unrelated responsibility is a signal to split it, not a convenient place to bolt on a new feature.
•	Stateless application tier: no service holds session or business state in process memory; horizontal scaling is adding another identical instance behind a load balancer, never a special case.
•	Configuration over redeployment: the pattern used throughout this document for classification hierarchies, attribute templates, and shift rosters — new business types, new units, new leave policies are data, not code — extends to infrastructure configuration (feature flags, tenant settings) as well.
•	Security and audit by construction: authentication, authorization, and the audit trail (Chapter 8, Appendix A.13) are cross-cutting concerns enforced at the platform level, not re-implemented per service.
•	Eventual consistency where it is genuinely acceptable: cross-service side effects (Section B.5) propagate asynchronously by default; only user-facing read-after-write operations within a single service are held to synchronous consistency.
Ten standard ASP.NET Core microservice best practices, and exactly where each one is implemented in this appendix — stated explicitly so that none of them are left as an unstated assumption behind the architecture:
B.1.1 Microservice Best-Practice Traceability
Best Practice	Implemented In
Design services around business capabilities, not technical layers	B.1 (Domain-aligned service boundaries); B.3's decomposition table, cut by bounded context (Item Master, HCM, Procurement…), never by technical function
Keep each service focused on a single responsibility	B.1.1 above (Single responsibility per service); enforced by B.3's one-service-one-bounded-context mapping
Prefer asynchronous messaging for long-running or decoupled workflows	B.1 (Eventual consistency); B.5.2 (MassTransit domain events), worked end to end in the EmployeeSeparationCleared trace
Use API versioning to support backward compatibility	B.1 (API-first); Chapter 10.1's /api/v1/ convention, carried into every service's ASP.NET Core Web API and gRPC contracts (B.11)
Containerize every service with Docker	B.11 (Containerization row — .NET native container publishing)
Orchestrate deployments using Kubernetes or Azure Container Apps	B.11 (Orchestration row)
Secure APIs with Microsoft Entra ID and OAuth 2.0	B.7.1 (Authentication & Authorization); B.11 (Authentication row)
Implement resilience patterns such as retries and circuit breakers	B.11 (Resilience row — Polly, wrapping every outbound gRPC/REST call and message publish)
Centralize logging and monitoring using Azure Monitor and OpenTelemetry	B.9 (Observability); B.11 (Observability row)
Automate builds, testing, and deployments with Azure DevOps or GitHub Actions	B.10 (CI/CD & Environment Strategy)
B.2 Logical Architecture Overview
Five layers, client requests flowing top to bottom and domain events flowing sideways through a shared event bus rather than service to service directly:
 
The Client Layer — Angular 18+ micro-frontends loaded independently per module via Module Federation (Finance, HCM, MES, and so on each their own deployable frontend, not one monolithic Angular app), .NET MAUI mobile apps, and external system integrations such as EDI or a third-party payroll provider — never calls a domain service directly. A request first passes through an edge zone (Cloudflare WAF/DDoS protection, then Kubernetes Ingress NGINX — Section B.7.2), then the API Gateway (Weavo.YarpGateway), which handles routing, authentication token validation, and rate limiting in one place rather than duplicating that logic in every service. The Gateway hands off to one of two Backend-for-Frontend services — Weavo.Bff.Web for the Angular micro-frontends, Weavo.Bff.Mobile for the MAUI apps, each aggregating and reshaping responses for its own client type over internal gRPC (Section B.3.4) — before reaching Domain Services, which implement the bounded contexts detailed in Section B.3. Shared Platform Services (authentication, notification, audit, document storage) are consumed by every domain service but owned by none of them. The Event Bus is how services learn about state changes in other services without a direct dependency — Section B.5 works through a concrete example, including the Transactional Outbox Pattern (Section B.5.3) that makes event delivery reliable. The Data Layer is polyglot (Section B.4.8): operational databases on whichever engine matches each service's workload, a cache for hot reference data, and a reporting store fed by replication rather than queried directly against production.
B.3 Service Decomposition & Domain Boundaries
The WeavoGo_Platform solution organizes its services into three tiers, each with a different reason to exist and a different rate of change: Platform Services (shared infrastructure every business module depends on but none of them owns), Master Data Services (the fine-grained masters this document has specified since Chapter 3), and Business Services (the ERP modules themselves, named after their SAP-equivalent module codes — FI, SD, MM, WM, MES, QM, PM, HCM, PS — since that naming convention is already familiar to anyone implementing or evaluating this platform against an established ERP vocabulary).
B.3.1 Platform Services
Service	Owns	SDS Coverage

DocumentManagement	File storage, versioning, previews	Chapter 7.4 (ItemDocument) metadata schema specified; this service now owns the physical file storage behind ItemDocument.FileURL (Section B.3.5)
Notification	Multi-channel dispatch (email, SMS, WebSockets, push)	Referenced conceptually since Section B.2/B.5 (Notification Service); no dedicated entity schema by design, per Section 1.2's transactional-document exclusion

B.3.4 Repository & Solution Structure
The actual .NET solution layout, one Domain/Application/Infrastructure/API project group per service (Section B.11.1), plus the shared and supporting areas every service depends on:
WeavoGo_Platform/
├── src/
│   ├── BuildingBlocks/              Shared kernel & cross-cutting libraries
│   ├── Gateways/                    Weavo.YarpGateway, Weavo.Bff.Web
│   ├── PlatformServices/            Identity, ApprovalEngine, DocumentManagement,
│   │                                Notification, AuditLogging, IntegrationGateway,
│   │                                AnalyticsReporting
│   ├── MasterDataServices/          OrganizationSubsystem, ItemMaster, CustomerMaster,
│   │                                VendorMaster, UomMaster, CurrencyMaster,
│   │                                CountryMaster, TaxMaster, SizeMaster,
│   │                                ColorMaster
│   └── BusinessServices/            WeavoFI, WeavoSD, WeavoMM, WeavoWM, WeavoMES,
│                                    WeavoQM, WeavoPM, WeavoHCM, WeavoPS
├── tests/                           ArchitectureTests, Shared test harnesses,
│                                    per-service unit/integration tests
├── deploy/                          k8s (Helm), docker-compose (local dev), terraform
├── docs/                            ADRs, OpenAPI specs, gRPC .proto definitions
├── Directory.Build.props            Shared .NET build settings
├── Directory.Packages.props         Central Package Management (unified NuGet versions)
└── WeavoGo_Platform.sln
•	BuildingBlocks is the shared kernel every service depends on, never the reverse: Weavo.BuildingBlocks.Kernel (domain primitives — Entity, AggregateRoot, ValueObject, IDomainEvent, the base types every service's Domain layer builds on per Section B.11.1); .Application (CQRS pipeline behaviors — validation, logging, performance, unit-of-work, wrapping every MediatR handler uniformly); .Infrastructure (EF Core interceptors for the audit and soft-delete patterns from Section 4.1, Redis cache, the Outbox pattern for reliable event publishing alongside Section B.5.2's MassTransit integration); .Messaging (the MassTransit/RabbitMQ wrappers referenced in Section B.11); .Observability (OpenTelemetry, structured logging, Prometheus metrics — Section B.9's traceId propagation lives here, once, not reimplemented per service); .Security (claims-principal extensions, multi-tenant context, service-to-service auth).
•	Gateways separates edge routing from response shaping, and separates response shaping itself by client type: Weavo.YarpGateway is the single edge proxy (token validation, rate limiting, dynamic route discovery via Consul/Kubernetes service discovery — Section B.11's API Gateway row); Weavo.Bff.Web aggregates and reshapes responses for the Angular micro-frontends, while Weavo.Bff.Mobile does the same for the MAUI apps with mobile-appropriate payload compression, both talking to domain services over internal gRPC rather than the public REST contract. No domain service ever needs to know what a particular frontend's screen happens to need, or which frontend is even asking — that shaping logic lives in the relevant BFF, not leaked backward into WeavoSD or ItemMaster.
•	ArchitectureTests, using NetArchTest, enforces Section B.11.1's Clean Architecture dependency rules — Infrastructure depends on Domain, never the reverse — as a failing build, not a code-review convention someone has to remember to check.
•	Directory.Packages.props applies Central Package Management across every service, so all nine Business Services and eight Master Data Services reference the same version of, for example, MassTransit or FluentValidation — a version drift between services is a build-time conflict, not a runtime surprise discovered in production.
B.3.5 Organizational Model Refinement: Tenant, Company, and Business Type
Two refinements to Section 5.2's Company/BusinessUnit model, made concrete by OrganizationSubsystem's project layout above, neither one contradicting the original design:
•	TenantMaster sits above Company, not in place of it. Section 5.2.1's Company remains the legal-entity layer (WeavoGo Group Ltd., That's It Sports Ware Ltd.); TenantId is now the outermost row-level-security boundary from Section B.6, with one or more CompanyId rows nested beneath a given TenantId. A group wanting WeavoGo and TISWL fully isolated from each other configures them as two tenants; a group wanting them to share reference data and cross-company reporting (Section 5.2.3's original worked example) configures them as two Companies under one tenant. The choice is configuration, not a schema change.
•	BusinessTypeMaster promotes BusinessUnit.BusinessType (a plain VARCHAR(50) column since Section 5.2.2) into a proper lookup table — the same classification-over-free-text discipline Chapter 3 applied to ItemCategory, now applied to the one column in the organizational model that had been left as text. Existing BusinessUnit rows are unaffected; BusinessTypeMaster simply gives “Manufacturing,” “Garments Production,” and “Embroidery” (Section 5.2.3) a governed, extensible list to be drawn from instead of free entry.
DocumentManagement and AuditLogging are the same kind of refinement applied at the platform-services tier: Chapter 7.4's ItemDocument and Chapter 8.3's ItemAuditLog were originally specified as tables living inside ItemMaster; they now keep their metadata schema exactly as specified, while the physical file storage (DocumentManagement) and the cross-service audit event stream (AuditLogging) are centralized, shared services that every other service's Infrastructure layer (Section B.3.4) calls into rather than reimplementing.
B.3.6 Coverage Roadmap
Where the repository structure is ahead of this document's schema chapters — stated plainly rather than left implicit, consistent with this appendix's practice of never presenting an unfinished area as complete:
Service / Area	Gap	Recommended Next Step
IntegrationGateway	No schema for EDI or banking (ISO20022/MT940) message mapping	A future chapter specifying inbound/outbound message types and field mappings
WeavoMES (work centers, IoT)	BOM schema exists (Section 6.5); work-center and shop-floor execution do not	A future chapter extending Chapter 6.5
WeavoPM	No schema at all	A future chapter — equipment master, work orders, preventive maintenance schedules
WeavoPS	No schema at all; ProjectId anticipated (Sections 9.3, A.1.2) but never modeled	A future chapter — project/WBS master, resource allocation, cost tracking against CostCenter
AnalyticsReporting	No schema of its own	None needed — confirmed by design (Section B.3.1), not a gap
B.4 Data Architecture
The relational schema specified across Chapters 4–13 and Appendix A is the logical data model regardless of deployment shape. Two physical deployment options are both valid against it:
•	Single shared database (recommended starting point): every table in one SQL Server instance, exactly as Chapter 4's DDL specifies. Simplest to operate, and sufficient for most single-region deployments; cross-module joins (e.g. reporting across Item Master and HCM) remain simple SQL.
•	Database-per-service (recommended once a service's independent scaling or release cadence genuinely requires it): each service in Section B.3 owns its own schema or database, and any data another service needs is obtained through that service's API or a replicated read model, never a cross-database join. This is the path to take when, for example, WeavoHCM needs to scale independently of ItemMaster under a very different load pattern.
Regardless of which option is chosen, three data-tier concerns apply uniformly:
•	Reference/master data that changes rarely and is read constantly (UOM, ItemCategory, LeaveType, ShiftMaster) is cached at the application tier with a short, explicit TTL, invalidated on write — never queried from the primary database on every request.
•	High-volume, append-mostly tables (ItemAttribute, ItemAuditLog, EmployeeAttribute) are the first candidates for partitioning by date or by tenant (Section B.6) as volume grows, since they are written far more often than the classification and configuration tables around them.
•	Reporting and analytics queries run against a replicated read store, never against the operational database directly — the same principle Section 6.1.2 already applied to WarehouseItem's cached balance, generalized to the whole platform.
B.4.1 Indexing Strategy
SQL Server does not automatically index foreign keys the way some other engines do, so every FK column specified since Chapter 4 needs an explicit non-clustered index — without one, every JOIN back to a parent table (ItemMaster to ItemFamily, WarehouseItem to Warehouse, EmployeeAttribute to Employee) forces a table scan on the child side as the table grows. Three further patterns apply on top of that baseline:
•	Every UNIQUE constraint already specified throughout this document (UQ_ItemMaster_Code, UQ_Employee_Code, and every other UQ_ constraint since Chapter 4) creates its supporting unique index automatically — no separate indexing decision needed for those columns.
•	Filtered indexes on IsDeleted = 0 (or IsActive = 1) for the tables whose queries almost always exclude soft-deleted rows — since Section 4.1's standard audit columns put IsDeleted on every table in the platform, an unfiltered index wastes space and I/O maintaining entries for rows no live query will ever match.
•	Covering indexes for the platform's highest-traffic read paths — the item search/list endpoint (Section 10.5) filters on ItemCategoryId, ItemStatus, and free-text against ItemCode/ItemName, so a composite index on (ItemCategoryId, ItemStatus) that includes ItemCode and ItemName lets that query satisfy itself entirely from the index without a key lookup back to the base table.
Table	Index	Type	Purpose
ItemMaster	(ItemCategoryId, ItemStatus) INCLUDE (ItemCode, ItemName)	Non-clustered, covering	Section 10.5's search/list endpoint
WarehouseItem	WarehouseId	Non-clustered	FK lookup; already unique-constrained on (ItemId, WarehouseId) for the other direction
ItemAttribute	ItemId	Non-clustered, filtered WHERE IsDeleted = 0	Every item-detail read (Section 10.4) joins here
EmployeeAttribute	EmployeeId	Non-clustered, filtered WHERE IsDeleted = 0	Same pattern as ItemAttribute, applied to Appendix A.3.4
ItemAuditLog	(ItemId, ChangedDate)	Non-clustered	Audit history lookups (Chapter 8.3) are always scoped to one item, ordered by date
B.4.2 Partitioning Strategy
The tables most likely to outgrow a single unpartitioned index are exactly the ones this document's never-hard-delete principle (Section 1.3) guarantees will grow without bound: audit logs, version history, and dynamic attribute values. SQL Server's native table partitioning splits each of these into manageable, independently-maintained ranges rather than one ever-growing table.
Table	Partition Key	Scheme	Rationale
ItemAuditLog	ChangedDate	Monthly range partitions	Write-heavy, append-only; old partitions become read-mostly and are the first candidates for archiving (Section B.4.6)
ItemVersion	CreatedDate	Monthly range partitions	Same growth pattern as ItemAuditLog (Section 8.4)
EmployeeAttribute	BusinessUnitId	Tenant-range partitions	A single large BusinessUnit's attribute volume should not degrade query performance for every other unit sharing the table
ItemAttribute	BusinessUnitId (via ItemMaster's owning entity) or ItemCategoryId	Tenant- or category-range partitions	Chosen per deployment based on which dimension actually dominates data skew; not fixed in advance
Partitioning by date additionally makes archiving (Section B.4.6) a metadata operation — switching a month-old partition out of the operational table — rather than a row-by-row DELETE against a live table under load.
B.4.3 Read Scaling: Replication & Reporting
SQL Server Always On Availability Groups provide both high availability (Section B.8's automated failover) and read scaling from the same secondary replicas: connections opened with ApplicationIntent=ReadOnly are automatically routed away from the primary, so reporting-style queries never compete with operational writes for the same I/O and lock resources.
•	The Reporting / Analytics Store in Section B.2's architecture diagram is either a read-only routed secondary replica (simplest, lowest additional operational cost) or a separately-modeled analytical schema fed by scheduled ETL, chosen per service based on how different its reporting query shape is from its operational one — a service whose reports are simple aggregations of its own tables needs no more than read-routing; one feeding cross-service dashboards benefits from a purpose-built analytical schema.
•	Every domain service's own read-heavy, non-reporting queries (e.g. the item search endpoint under normal load) still run against the primary or a synchronous secondary, not the reporting store — Section B.4's reporting-store principle applies to actual reporting and analytics workloads, not to a service's everyday read traffic.
B.4.4 Concurrency Control
RowVersion (Section 4.1's standard audit columns, present on every table in the platform) is the optimistic-concurrency mechanism throughout, not merely an audit artifact. EF Core maps it to a [Timestamp] property and automatically includes it in every UPDATE statement's WHERE clause — a concurrent modification that changed RowVersion since the current request read the row causes the UPDATE to affect zero rows, which EF Core surfaces as a DbUpdateConcurrencyException rather than silently overwriting someone else's change.
•	Optimistic concurrency was chosen over pessimistic (row-locking) concurrency because the platform's write pattern — many short, independent transactions from warehouse clerks, sewing-floor supervisors, and HR staff working against largely disjoint rows — would pay a constant lock-contention cost under pessimistic locking for conflicts that, in practice, rarely happen.
•	A caught DbUpdateConcurrencyException is surfaced to the caller as a dedicated error code, following the same standard error envelope as every other failure mode in Chapter 12.2, rather than a generic 500-level failure the caller cannot act on.
B.4.5 Query Performance for Analytical Workloads
•	Columnstore indexes on the reporting store's largest fact-like tables (transaction-adjacent data, attribute value history) — column-oriented storage and batch-mode execution suit the wide aggregations a dashboard or export runs far better than the row-oriented indexes B.4.1 specifies for operational lookups.
•	Query Store is enabled on every production database, giving each service's own query plan history rather than relying on a DBA's memory of “what used to be fast” — a plan regression after a deployment is visible and reversible (forcing the prior plan) without waiting for a full investigation.
•	Analytical and operational workloads are never served from the same compute tier under load — Section B.4.3's read-routing already isolates them at the connection level; Resource Governor (or an equivalent workload-management feature) is the backstop that prevents one heavy ad hoc report from starving operational throughput even on a shared secondary.
B.4.6 Data Retention & Archiving
Never-hard-delete (Section 1.3) is a correctness requirement, not a performance one — it does not follow that every historical row must stay in the fastest, most expensive storage tier forever. Partitioning (Section B.4.2) is what makes the distinction between “retained” and “operationally hot” practical to act on:
•	A partition older than a policy-defined retention threshold (itself configuration, not a hard-coded cutoff — the same discipline this document has applied to every other policy since Chapter 3) is switched out of the operational table into an archive table on cheaper storage, a metadata-only operation rather than a bulk delete.
•	Archived data remains queryable — through a view that unions the operational and archive tables, or through a federated query — so Chapter 8.3's audit-trail requirement and Appendix A's compliance needs are satisfied without permanently paying the operational table's storage and index-maintenance cost for a five-year-old audit record nobody has queried in years.
This is the same principle as Section 6.1.2's WarehouseItem cache one layer further out: keep the fast path fast by moving what does not need to be there, while never actually discarding it — archiving is where the platform's storage tier is what changes, never the retention guarantee itself.
B.4.7 Connection Management & Resiliency
•	EF Core's DbContextPool reuses DbContext instances across requests within a service, avoiding the per-request setup cost of establishing a fresh context and its underlying connection from the pool.
•	Every database call is wrapped in EF Core's EnableRetryOnFailure, layered with the same Polly resilience policies Section B.11 already applies to outbound gRPC/REST calls — a transient SQL error (a brief network blip, a deadlock victim) is retried automatically rather than surfaced as a user-facing failure on the first attempt.
•	Write connections target the primary replica; read connections for reporting-style queries are opened with ApplicationIntent=ReadOnly (Section B.4.3) — this routing decision is made once, in each service's data-access configuration, not repeated ad hoc at every call site.
B.4.8 Polyglot Persistence Strategy
Three engines, each earning its place for a specific, narrow reason rather than a workload-by-workload judgment call per service: SQL Server is the default for every bounded context with committed, relational business data — which, after the two exceptions below, is nearly everything in Section B.3. Sections B.4.1–B.4.7's indexing, partitioning, and concurrency techniques apply directly, without qualification, to that entire majority.
Engine	Scope	Rationale
Microsoft SQL Server	Every bounded context in Section B.3 except WeavoMES and the Draft & Session Store below — including ItemMaster, all Master Data Services, and all remaining Business Services (WeavoFI, WeavoSD, WeavoMM, WeavoWM, WeavoQM, WeavoPM, WeavoHCM, WeavoPS)	Strict relational integrity for committed business data: ACID compliance, foreign key constraints, multi-table joins — exactly the workload Chapters 4–13 and Appendix A's schema and Chapter 10.9's T-SQL DDL specify directly, with no reconciliation needed for any service assigned here
PostgreSQL (with TimescaleDB)	WeavoMES only	Shop-floor execution is the one genuinely time-series-shaped workload in the platform: real-time IoT/MQTT sensor streaming and machine telemetry from work centers, suited to TimescaleDB's hypertables in a way no other bounded context's data is
MongoDB	A single shared Draft & Session Store (Section B.4.8.1), not owned by any one business service	Auto-saved, in-progress form data and UI session state, deliberately never used for committed business data — the variable, form-by-form document shape is exactly what a schemaless store is for, and nothing here needs relational integrity because nothing here is a system of record
Redis Cluster (Sentinel)	Shared across every service via BuildingBlocks.Infrastructure	Short-lived, ephemeral state distinct from MongoDB's durable drafts: auth token cache, rate-limiting buckets at the YARP gateway, the SignalR backplane, and Outbox deduplication locks (Section B.5.3)
B.4.8.1 The Draft & Session Store
Any create or edit form, in any service, can auto-save its current field values as the user types — not as a committed row anywhere, but as a working draft that follows the user across devices. Logging in on a different device and reopening the same form shows the same in-progress content, because the draft lives centrally in MongoDB rather than in that one browser tab's local state.
•	This is a genuinely different concept from ItemMaster's own Draft status (Section 8.1) or the “Save as Draft” button on the item creation wireframe (Section 11.2.1). Those are committed rows in SQL Server, fully subject to Chapter 4's constraints, simply sitting in an early lifecycle state. The MongoDB working draft is what exists before that — unsaved, un-submitted, disposable content that only becomes a real row once the user actually saves or submits the form through the normal Chapter 10 API contract.
•	A single collection shape serves every form in every service, since the whole point is not needing a schema per form type: a document is keyed by (UserAccountId, FormType, EntityId), carries the current field values as an arbitrary payload, and expires automatically — a MongoDB TTL index removes an abandoned draft after a policy-defined period (e.g. 30 days) without any service having to clean up after itself.
The document shape, worked through the same Dell Latitude 7450 example used since Section 3.8 — here, an in-progress edit rather than a committed row:
{
  "_id": "draft:101:ItemMaster.Edit:6",
  "userAccountId": 101,
  "formType": "ItemMaster.Edit",
  "entityId": 6,
  "draftPayload": {
    "itemName": "Dell Latitude 7450 Laptop",
    "attributes": { "RAM": 32, "WARRANTY": 3 },
    "changeReason": "RAM configuration corrected from 16GB to 32GB post-launch"
  },
  "lastSavedDate": "2026-01-20T14:32:00Z",
  "expiresAt": "2026-02-19T14:32:00Z"
}
Nothing in this document changes the entity that draft describes — Section 8.4's ItemVersion, EmployeeCompensation's effective-dating, and every other rule in Chapters 4–13 and Appendix A apply exactly as specified the moment this draft is actually submitted. The Draft & Session Store only ever holds the content on its way there.
B.4.8.2 Cross-Store Query Implications
•	WeavoMES's PostgreSQL data joins with any SQL Server-hosted service (e.g. a BillOfMaterial in WeavoMES referencing an ItemId owned by ItemMaster) only through each service's own API (Section B.5.1) at request time, or through AnalyticsReporting's replicated copy for reporting-scale volumes — never a live cross-database join.
•	The Draft & Session Store is never queried for reporting or joined against anything — by design, it holds no committed data, so there is nothing in it a report could correctly need.
B.5 Integration Architecture
Two integration modes, used for two different purposes rather than interchangeably: synchronous request/response for anything a caller needs an immediate answer to, and asynchronous events for anything another service needs to react to but the original caller does not need to wait for.
B.5.1 Synchronous: REST APIs
The API contract specified in Chapter 10 — versioned paths, the standard error envelope, idempotent writes — is the synchronous integration layer. External callers — the web app, the mobile app, third-party integrations — always use this REST contract, served by ASP.NET Core Web API. Service-to-service calls inside the platform (e.g. WeavoMES validating an ItemId with ItemMaster before creating a BillOfMaterial) use gRPC instead (Section B.11), for lower latency and a strongly-typed .proto contract; the gRPC contract for a service's internal API mirrors its REST contract field-for-field, so the two never drift into two different views of the same data.
B.5.2 Asynchronous: Domain Events
A domain service publishes an event to the Event Bus — MassTransit, riding on RabbitMQ or Azure Service Bus (Section B.11) — whenever a state transition happens that another service might care about, without knowing or caring who, if anyone, is listening. Each event is a versioned MassTransit message contract (a plain C# record shared via a small contracts package, not the full entity), so a consumer depends only on the fields it actually needs. Representative events already implied by business rules elsewhere in this document:
Event	Published By	Typically Consumed By
ItemActivated	ItemMaster	AnalyticsReporting, WeavoMM (item becomes orderable)
ApprovalStepCompleted	ApprovalEngine	Whichever service raised the original request (ItemMaster, WeavoHCM)
EmployeeSeparationCleared	WeavoHCM	Identity (disable UserAccount), ItemMaster (revert issued ItemSerial)
LeaveEncashmentProcessed	WeavoHCM	WeavoFI (post payment), Notification
ShiftRosterChanged	WeavoHCM	WeavoHCM's own payroll module (recompute allowances)
The EmployeeSeparationCleared row above is worth tracing end to end, since Appendix A.10.3 already described the underlying data relationship: when an EmployeeClearanceItem line closes referencing an issued ItemSerial, WeavoHCM publishes EmployeeSeparationCleared; ItemMaster, subscribed to that event, reverts the referenced ItemSerial.Status to InStock without the two services ever calling each other synchronously or sharing a database — each service's data stays internally consistent, and the cross-module effect described in Appendix A.10.3's note is what actually carries it out at runtime.
B.5.3 Reliability: The Transactional Outbox Pattern
Section B.5.2's domain events are only as reliable as the gap between “the database transaction committed” and “the event was published” is small. Publishing directly from application code after committing a transaction leaves exactly that gap: a crash between the two steps silently drops the event, even though the underlying state change is safely persisted. The Transactional Outbox Pattern closes it by making the event itself part of the same local transaction as the business data it describes.
[ Domain Operation ]
        │  BEGIN TRANSACTION
        ├─▶  Save aggregate changes (e.g. EmployeeSeparation, ItemMaster)
        └─▶  Save Outbox message record, same transaction, same database
        │  COMMIT TRANSACTION
        ↓
[ Outbox Background Processor ]  (Quartz.NET scheduled job, or a .NET Hosted Service)
        │  polls for unpublished Outbox rows
        ↓
[ RabbitMQ / Kafka Event Bus ]  (Section B.5.2)
        │
        ↓
[ Downstream Consumer Microservice ]  — executes idempotently, deduplicated via Redis
•	The business state and the Outbox message row commit or roll back together, in one local ACID transaction — there is no window where the aggregate changed but the event was lost, or the event fired but the aggregate change didn't actually commit.
•	A background processor — a Quartz.NET scheduled job or a lightweight .NET Hosted Service, whichever a given service's BuildingBlocks.Infrastructure configuration already runs (Section B.3.4) — polls the Outbox table for unpublished rows and hands them to MassTransit for actual delivery, marking each row published only after the broker confirms receipt.
•	Because the background processor can itself fail after publishing but before marking a row published, delivery is at-least-once, not exactly-once — a message can be published twice. Consumers execute idempotently, checking a message identifier against a short-lived Redis deduplication key (Section B.4.8's Redis Cluster row) before acting on it a second time, rather than assuming the broker guarantees single delivery.
This pattern applies identically regardless of which polyglot store (Section B.4.8) a given service uses for its own aggregate data — the Outbox table itself lives in that same service's own database, relational or document, since the whole point is a single local transaction covering both the aggregate and the outgoing event.
B.6 Multi-Tenancy & Multi-Company Architecture
Company and BusinessUnit (Section 5.2) are the tenancy boundary this platform already models at the data level — WeavoGo Group Ltd. and That's It Sports Ware Ltd. (Section 5.2.3) are two tenants of the same deployed application, not two separate deployments.
Model	Description	Chosen for This Platform?
Separate deployment per tenant	Each Company gets its own application instance and database	No — operationally expensive at the scale this platform targets, and defeats cross-company rollup reporting (Section 5.2.3)
Separate database per tenant, shared application	One application codebase, one database per Company	No — still complicates cross-company reporting; considered only if a future customer requires physical data isolation
Shared database, row-level tenancy (chosen)	One application, one database; every tenant-scoped table carries CompanyId or BusinessUnitId directly	Yes — matches the schema exactly as specified since Chapter 5, and keeps cross-unit/cross-company rollups (Section 5.2.3, Section 9.3.2) as ordinary SQL rather than cross-database queries
Row-level tenancy places two obligations on every service: every query must be scoped by the caller's authorized BusinessUnitId(s) (Section 12.3's security matrix already specifies this at the permission level; the architecture obligation is enforcing it at the query level, not trusting the client to ask correctly), and no table that should be tenant-scoped may be created without a BusinessUnitId or CompanyId column — a gap here is a data leak between tenants, not merely a missing feature.
B.7 Security Architecture
B.7.1 Authentication & Authorization
UserAccount (Section A.12.2) is the identity record; credential verification itself is delegated to Microsoft Entra ID via OAuth 2.0 / OpenID Connect, not re-implemented inside the platform. Every service, and the API Gateway in front of them, validates the resulting JWT bearer token issued by Entra ID using the standard ASP.NET Core JWT Bearer authentication middleware — the same validation logic and the same token, whether the call originated from the web app, the mobile app, or one service calling another over gRPC (Section B.5.1). The token carries the caller's UserAccountId and resolved UserRole grants (Section A.12.3) as claims, so downstream services check authorization from the token itself, without a database round-trip on every call.
Authorization is enforced twice, deliberately redundantly: at the API Gateway (coarse-grained — does this token have any grant for this endpoint at all) and again inside the owning service (fine-grained — does this specific UserRole/BusinessUnitId combination permit this specific action on this specific row), matching Chapter 12's security matrix and its “scoped” permissions exactly.
B.7.2 Network Zones
Four network tiers, each only reachable from the one immediately above it:
•	Edge zone: Cloudflare WAF and DDoS protection in front of the Kubernetes Ingress NGINX Controller — the only layer with a public IP; every request is filtered for known attack patterns before it ever reaches cluster-internal networking.
•	Public zone: the API Gateway (Section B.11's Weavo.YarpGateway) and the BFF layer (Section B.3.4) only, reachable from the edge zone.
•	Application zone: domain services, platform services, the event bus. Reachable only from the public zone and from each other.
•	Data zone: operational databases across all three polyglot engines (Section B.4.8), cache, reporting store. Reachable only from the application zone — no service outside this zone, including the API Gateway, ever holds a direct database connection.
B.7.3 Encryption & Secrets
•	TLS in transit for every connection, public zone through to the data zone, with no exceptions for “internal-only” traffic.
•	Encryption at rest for every operational database and backup, using the underlying platform's native database encryption rather than application-level encryption of individual columns, except where a column's sensitivity specifically warrants it (e.g. Person.NationalIdNumber, Section A.3.1).
•	Secrets (database credentials, signing keys, third-party API keys) live in a dedicated secrets manager, never in application configuration files or source control, and are rotated on a defined schedule rather than only on suspected compromise.
B.8 Scalability, Performance & High Availability
Every domain service in Section B.3 is stateless, so the default scaling response to load is horizontal — more instances behind the load balancer — rather than vertical resizing of a single instance.
Concern	Approach
Read-heavy reference data	Application-tier cache (Section B.4) with short TTL, invalidated on write
Write-heavy append tables	Partitioned by date or BusinessUnitId once volume warrants it (Section B.4.2 details the specific tables and partition keys)
Reporting load	Isolated to a read-routed replica or dedicated analytical schema (Section B.4.3); never competes with operational transaction throughput
Service-level scaling	Independent horizontal scaling per service in Section B.3, sized to that service's own load profile rather than one shared scaling policy for the whole platform
High availability	Multi-availability-zone deployment for the application tier and the primary database, with automated failover
Disaster recovery	Defined RPO/RTO targets per data tier — tighter for operational databases than for the reporting store, which can be rebuilt from replication
B.9 Observability
Every request entering at the API Gateway is assigned a traceId — the same field already present in Section 10.8's standard error envelope — and that traceId propagates through every synchronous call and every published event the request triggers, so a single support ticket referencing one traceId can reconstruct the full cross-service path a request took, not just which service happened to return the error.
•	Centralized, structured logging from every service, correlated by traceId, retained long enough to investigate an incident after the fact rather than only while it is active.
•	Metrics (request rate, error rate, latency percentiles) per service and per endpoint, feeding automated alerting on defined thresholds rather than relying on someone noticing a dashboard.
•	Distributed tracing across service boundaries and across the event bus, so a slow EmployeeSeparationCleared handler (Section B.5.2) is visible as a distinct span, not folded into “the leave service was slow” generically.
B.10 CI/CD & Environment Strategy
Environment	Purpose	Promotion Gate
Development	Active feature development; ephemeral, may be reset at any time	Automated tests pass
QA	Integration testing across services; stable enough for manual test execution	QA sign-off
UAT	Business stakeholder acceptance against realistic data volumes	UAT sign-off (mirrors Chapter 8's approval discipline, applied to releases rather than items)
Production	Live system	Change-control approval; deployed via the same pipeline as every lower environment, never a manual hotfix path
Every service's pipeline — restore, build, run unit and Testcontainers-based integration tests (Section B.11), publish the container image, deploy — is automated end to end in Azure DevOps or GitHub Actions, the same choice of either tool applying consistently across all twenty-four services rather than mixing tooling per team. Infrastructure is defined as code and applied through that same pipeline as application code, so an environment's configuration is reconstructable from source control rather than accumulated through manual changes over time. Production releases use a blue-green or canary rollout — the new version serves a small fraction of traffic first, with automated rollback on an elevated error rate, rather than a single all-at-once cutover.
B.11 Technology Stack: .NET Microservices
The platform is built as a set of ASP.NET Core microservices on .NET — not a recommendation among options, as earlier drafts of this appendix framed it, but the chosen implementation stack. Every service in Section B.3's decomposition table is a separate ASP.NET Core Web API project, independently built, tested, containerized, and deployed, sharing a common set of libraries for the cross-cutting concerns (authentication, validation, logging) so that no service reinvents them.
Layer	Technology	Rationale
Runtime	.NET 8 (LTS)	Long-term support release; every service targets the same LTS version so the platform upgrades on one schedule, not per-service
Service framework	ASP.NET Core Web API (Minimal APIs for simple endpoints, Controllers where a richer filter/model-binding pipeline is needed)	One framework across all nine services in Section B.3; matches Chapter 10's REST contract directly
Inter-service synchronous calls	gRPC (via Grpc.AspNetCore) for service-to-service calls; ASP.NET Core Web API (REST) for anything a browser, mobile app, or external system calls	gRPC's contract-first .proto definitions and binary payload suit high-frequency internal calls (e.g. Manufacturing Service validating an ItemId); external callers keep the REST contract from Chapter 10 unchanged
Data access	Entity Framework Core (Code-First, migrations generated from the entity model that mirrors Chapter 10.9's DDL exactly)	Keeps the schema and the C# entity classes from drifting apart; EF Core migrations are the authoritative history of schema changes in each service's database
High-throughput read queries	Dapper, used selectively for reporting-style queries where EF Core's change-tracking overhead is unnecessary	Section B.4's reporting store and any hot read path benefit from a thinner data-access layer without giving up EF Core everywhere else
Validation	FluentValidation	Implements Chapter 12.1's validation matrix as executable validator classes, one per entity, reviewable against that matrix row by row
In-process mediator / CQRS	MediatR	Separates each endpoint's HTTP concerns from its business logic, keeping the Application layer (Section B.11.1) testable independently of ASP.NET Core itself
Resilience	Polly	Retry, circuit-breaker, and timeout policies wrapped around every outbound gRPC/REST call and message publish, so one slow dependency degrades gracefully instead of cascading
Event bus abstraction	MassTransit, over RabbitMQ (self-hosted, default) or Apache Kafka (for ultra-high-volume topics), with the Transactional Outbox Pattern (Section B.5.3) guaranteeing delivery	Section B.5.2's domain events are published and consumed through MassTransit's typed message contracts; the Outbox pattern closes the gap between committing state and publishing the event that describes it
API Gateway	YARP (Yet Another Reverse Proxy), with dynamic route discovery via Consul or native Kubernetes service discovery	A .NET-native reverse proxy keeps the gateway on the same runtime and observability stack as every service behind it; dynamic discovery means a new service instance is routable without a gateway redeploy
Authentication	Microsoft Entra ID (OAuth 2.0 / OpenID Connect), tokens validated via ASP.NET Core JWT Bearer middleware	Section B.7.1's chosen identity provider; every service and the API Gateway validate the same Entra ID-issued token via the same standard middleware, with no per-service credential logic
Caching	Redis via StackExchange.Redis / Microsoft.Extensions.Caching.StackExchangeRedis	Backs Section B.4's reference-data cache; the distributed cache abstraction in ASP.NET Core makes the same code portable to an in-memory cache for local development
Database	Polyglot: Microsoft SQL Server, PostgreSQL (with TimescaleDB), MongoDB — see Section B.4.8 for which service uses which	No single engine fits every bounded context; Section B.4.8 assigns each service the engine matching its actual workload rather than defaulting everything to one
Containerization	Docker, built via .NET's native container support (dotnet publish /t:PublishContainer)	No separate Dockerfile-authoring step per service; the container image is a build output like any other
Orchestration	Kubernetes (Helm charts per service), or Azure Container Apps for services that do not need Kubernetes' full feature set	Matches Section B.8's stateless, horizontally-scaled service model either way; Azure Container Apps trades some Kubernetes flexibility for materially less operational overhead, a reasonable default for a smaller initial deployment
Observability	OpenTelemetry .NET SDK, exported to Azure Monitor	Section B.9's traceId propagates automatically through ASP.NET Core's built-in distributed tracing instrumentation, gRPC, and MassTransit; Azure Monitor is the default backend for logs, metrics, and traces, with the OpenTelemetry SDK keeping that choice swappable rather than hard-wired into application code
Testing	xUnit for unit tests; Testcontainers for integration tests against a real, ephemeral SQL Server and RabbitMQ/Azure Service Bus emulator	Integration tests run against the actual technologies in this table, not mocks of them, catching schema and messaging contract drift before deployment
Web frontend	Angular 18+ micro-frontends via Module Federation, one deployable bundle per business module (Finance, HCM, MES, …)	A module ships and rolls back independently of every other module's frontend, matching the independent-deployability of the backend services behind it
Mobile frontend	.NET MAUI	One C# codebase for the mobile client, sharing DTOs and validation logic with the backend's .NET services where practical
Edge security	Cloudflare WAF / DDoS protection, in front of Kubernetes Ingress NGINX Controller	Section B.7.2's edge zone — the only layer with a public IP; filters known attack patterns before traffic reaches cluster-internal networking
B.11.1 Per-Service Internal Architecture: Clean Architecture
Every service in Section B.3 is Clean Architecture, without exception and without a lighter-weight alternative for smaller services — the same four layers, the same Dependency Rule, verified by the same ArchitectureTests project (Section B.3.4, ADR-012) rather than left as a convention a developer could reasonably deviate from under deadline pressure. The rule that actually matters is not the layer names; it is which direction a project reference is allowed to point:
Layer	Responsibility	May Reference	Must Never Reference
Domain	Entities, aggregates, value objects, domain events, and business rules — the direct C# translation of Chapters 4–13 and Appendix A's schema and constraints	Nothing outside the .NET base class library	Application, Infrastructure, API, or any framework package (EF Core, ASP.NET Core, MediatR, MassTransit) — Domain has zero framework dependencies
Application	Use cases: CQRS commands and queries, FluentValidation rules, orchestration of Domain operations; defines the interfaces Infrastructure will implement	Domain only	Infrastructure, API, or any concrete persistence/messaging technology — Application depends on abstractions (e.g. IItemRepository) it declares, never on EF Core or MassTransit directly
Infrastructure	Implements the interfaces Domain/Application declare: EF Core DbContext and repositories, MassTransit consumers, gRPC clients to other services, Redis access, the Outbox writer	Application, Domain	API — nothing in Infrastructure is ever called directly by a Controller or Minimal API handler; it is only ever reached through a Domain/Application-declared interface
API / Presentation	Translates HTTP/gRPC requests into Application commands/queries and responses back; owns the composition root that wires concrete Infrastructure classes to the interfaces they implement	Application directly; Infrastructure only inside the composition root (startup wiring), never inside a request-handling code path	Domain directly — API never touches an entity except through an Application command/query result
The same four layers, drawn as the request path a call actually takes — which is not the same thing as the dependency direction the table above states, a distinction worth being explicit about:
[ API / Presentation ]  ASP.NET Core Controller or Minimal API endpoint
        │  1. receives the request, maps it to a Command or Query
        ↓
[ Application ]  MediatR handler, FluentValidation validators
        │  2. validates, orchestrates, calls Domain logic through an interface it declares
        ↓
[ Domain ]  Entities, aggregates, business rules — pure C#, no I/O
        │  3. Domain returns a result; it never calls outward to fetch or persist anything itself
        ↓
[ Infrastructure ]  EF Core / MassTransit / gRPC — implements the interface Application declared
Step 3 is the one call-flow arrows alone make easy to misread: Domain does not call Infrastructure. Application calls an interface (e.g. IItemRepository.GetByIdAsync) that Domain or Application itself declares; Infrastructure is simply whichever concrete class the composition root registered against that interface at startup. The request appears to “flow down” to Infrastructure, but the compile-time dependency — the thing ArchitectureTests actually checks — points the opposite way: Infrastructure depends on Domain and Application, never the reverse. This is the Dependency Inversion Principle, and it is the entire reason Domain can be unit-tested with no database, no message broker, and no HTTP pipeline running at all.
B.11.2 NetArchTest Assertions
ADR-012 committed to enforcing this as a failing build, not a review checklist. The specific assertions ArchitectureTests (Section B.3.4) runs against every service, on every build:
•	Domain must not have a dependency on any other project in the service — asserted directly against the compiled assembly's referenced assemblies, not inferred from folder structure.
•	Application must depend only on Domain — a reference from Application to Infrastructure, or to EF Core, ASP.NET Core, or any other framework package, fails the build immediately.
•	Infrastructure classes must not be referenced from API — a Controller instantiating a DbContext or a MassTransit consumer directly, instead of resolving an interface through dependency injection, fails the build.
•	No class outside the composition root (Program.cs) may reference concrete types from more than one layer simultaneously — the composition root is the sole, deliberate exception to the Dependency Rule, and ArchitectureTests treats any other class doing the same as a violation.
This layering and this exact set of assertions are identical across all twenty-four services in Section B.3 — WeavoHCM's Domain layer contains ShiftMaster, ShiftGroup, and ShiftRoster exactly as specified in Appendix A.7.1–A.7.2, checked by the same ArchitectureTests rules as ItemMaster's Domain layer. A developer moving from one service to another is learning new domain rules and a new NetArchTest pass/fail baseline that was already green, not a new architecture to reverse-engineer from the code.
B.11.3 Worked Example: WeavoHCM Class Names Per Layer
WeavoHCM owns the largest single Domain layer in the platform — over forty tables across Appendix A. Rather than list all of them, the four representative sub-areas below are the ones this document has already worked concrete examples through (Rehana Begum's roster, Karim Hossain's leave carry-forward, Jahangir Alam's separation), so the class names below can be read against scenarios already established rather than in the abstract.
Sub-Area	Domain	Application	Infrastructure
Person & Employment	Person, Employee, Designation, JobFamily, JobGrade, PayGrade (entities); EmploymentType (value object wrapping Section A.3.2's CHECK constraint)	CreateEmployeeCommand, RehireEmployeeCommand, GetEmployeeByIdQuery; CreateEmployeeCommandValidator (implements Section 12.1 row-by-row)	HcmDbContext, EmployeeConfiguration : IEntityTypeConfiguration<Employee>, EmployeeRepository : IEmployeeRepository
Shift & Roster	ShiftMaster, ShiftSegment, ShiftGroup, ShiftGroupMember, ShiftRoster, ShiftRosterPattern, EmployeeShiftRosterAssignment, EmployeeShiftOverride, ShiftAllowance	ResolveEmployeeShiftForDateQuery, RecordShiftOverrideCommand; IShiftRosterResolver (the interface Application declares for the modular-arithmetic cycle resolution in Section A.7.2)	ShiftRosterResolver : IShiftRosterResolver (the concrete cycle-day/cycle-week math), ShiftRosterChangedPublisher (Outbox writer for Section B.5.2's event)
Leave	LeaveType, LeavePolicy, EmployeeLeaveBalance, LeaveEncashment; LeaveYearWindow (value object wrapping LeaveYearStartDate/EndDate and the CalendarYear/ServiceYear computation from Section A.7.10)	CloseLeaveYearWindowCommand (the carry-forward/encashment/lapse decision from Section A.7.12), GetEmployeeLeaveBalanceQuery; ILeaveEncashmentRepository	LeaveEncashmentRepository : ILeaveEncashmentRepository, EmployeeLeaveBalanceConfiguration (maps the CK_EmployeeLeaveBalance_Formula check from Section A.7.12 as an EF Core value conversion plus a database CHECK constraint in the generated migration)
Separation & Settlement	SeparationType, EmployeeSeparation, EmployeeClearanceItem, FinalSettlementComponent, EmployeeFinalSettlement	RecordEmployeeSeparationCommand, CloseClearanceItemCommand, ApproveFinalSettlementCommand	EmployeeSeparationClearedPublisher (Outbox writer for the event Section B.5.2 traces into ItemMaster reverting an ItemSerial to InStock)
API / Presentation for all four sub-areas, matching Chapter 10's endpoint conventions directly:
•	EmployeesController — POST /api/v1/employees, GET /api/v1/employees/{id}, matching Section 10.3–10.4's shape
•	ShiftRostersController — GET /api/v1/employees/{id}/shift?date={date}, POST /api/v1/shift-overrides
•	LeaveController — GET /api/v1/employees/{id}/leave-balance, POST /api/v1/leave-encashments
•	SeparationsController — POST /api/v1/separations, POST /api/v1/separations/{id}/clearance-items/{itemId}/close
Every class above sits in exactly the layer Section B.11.1's table permits: ShiftRosterResolver, the one piece of genuinely non-trivial logic in this slice (the cycle-day and cycle-week arithmetic from Section A.7.2–A.7.3), lives in Infrastructure only because it is the concrete implementation of IShiftRosterResolver — the interface itself, and the decision of when to call it, belongs to Application. Moving the arithmetic into Domain directly would fail ArchitectureTests the moment Domain referenced a date/time provider abstraction Infrastructure owns; keeping it in Infrastructure behind an interface is what keeps Domain's EmployeeShiftRosterAssignment entity unit-testable with no clock and no database.
B.11.4 Worked Example: Master Data Services Class Names
ItemMaster is the flagship Master Data Service — the most structurally complex, with its own dynamic attribute engine — so it is the one shown as a full worked example across all four layers; the others are noted at the depth Section B.3.2's coverage actually supports, some with a full schema behind them and a shorter “same pattern” note, and any genuinely still-unspecified service (Section B.3.6) left honestly blank rather than filled with invented class names.
Layer	ItemMaster Classes
Domain	ItemCategory, ItemGroup, ItemSubGroup, ItemFamily, ItemMaster, AttributeDefinition, AttributeTemplate, TemplateAttribute, ItemAttribute (entities, Sections 4.3–4.11); ItemCode, AttributeValue (value objects wrapping the CK_ItemAttribute_SingleValue discriminated-union rule from Section 4.11)
Application	CreateItemCommand, UpdateItemCommand, SubmitItemForApprovalCommand (Sections 10.3, 10.6–10.7); GetItemDetailQuery, SearchItemsQuery (Sections 10.4–10.5); CreateItemCommandValidator, UpdateItemCommandValidator (FluentValidation, implementing Section 12.1's matrix rows 1–7 directly — e.g. CK_ItemMaster_StockFlags becomes a single validator rule, not a comment referencing the chapter); IItemRepository, IAttributeTemplateRepository
Infrastructure	ItemMasterDbContext, ItemConfiguration : IEntityTypeConfiguration<ItemMaster> (an EF Core Fluent API translation of Section 4.10's schema table, column for column), ItemRepository : IItemRepository, ItemActivatedPublisher (Outbox writer for Section B.5.2's event); EF Core migrations generated from this configuration are the same DDL as Section 10.9.8, not a hand-maintained duplicate of it
API / Presentation	ItemsController — endpoints matching Section 10.2's catalogue exactly: POST /items, GET /items/{itemId}, GET /items, PATCH /items/{itemId}, POST /items/{itemId}/submit
The other Master Data Services, at their current coverage level (Section B.3.2, B.3.6):
Service	Domain Classes	Coverage
OrganizationSubsystem	Company, BusinessUnit, Department, CostCenter, Warehouse (Sections 5.2, A.1.1–A.1.2, 6.1.1)	Fully specified; Application/Infrastructure/API follow the same pattern as ItemMaster above
VendorMaster	Supplier, SupplierItem, SupplierPrice (Section 6.2)	Fully specified under its enterprise name; same pattern
UomMaster	UOM, UOMConversion (Sections 5.4–5.5)	Fully specified; same pattern
TaxMaster	TaxMapping (Section 6.4.2)	Fully specified; same pattern
CustomerMaster	Customer, CustomerAddress, Address (Appendix C.3–C.4)	Fully specified; same pattern as ItemMaster above
CurrencyMaster	Currency, ExchangeRate (Appendix C.2)	Fully specified; same pattern
CountryMaster	Country, AdministrativeDivisionType, AdministrativeDivision (Appendix C.1)	Fully specified; same pattern
CustomerMaster, CurrencyMaster, and CountryMaster were unresolved gaps as recently as the previous revision of this appendix — Appendix C closed all three, and the Domain classes above reflect that directly rather than being retrofitted as placeholders. Section B.3.6's roadmap is updated accordingly; IntegrationGateway, WeavoMES's work-center/IoT scope, WeavoPM, and WeavoPS remain the honest gaps still outstanding.
B.12 Architecture Decision Records
Short-form records of the decisions this appendix makes, in the format a future architect revisiting one of them would expect.
ADR	Decision	Rationale (see Section)
001	One logical relational schema; physical deployment (shared DB vs. database-per-service) is a separate, later decision	Section B.4 — avoids coupling the data model's correctness to an infrastructure choice that may change with scale
002	Cross-service side effects propagate via asynchronous domain events, not synchronous service-to-service calls	Section B.5 — keeps services independently deployable and avoids cascading failure when one service is slow
003	Row-level multi-tenancy (CompanyId/BusinessUnitId on tenant-scoped tables), not database-per-tenant	Section B.6 — preserves cross-unit/cross-company rollup reporting already required since Section 5.2.3
004	API-first: internal and external callers use the identical REST contract from Chapter 10	Section B.1, B.5.1 — prevents a private internal protocol from silently diverging from the documented public one
005	Authorization enforced at both the API Gateway and the owning service, not either alone	Section B.7.1 — defense in depth; a gateway misconfiguration is not a full authorization bypass
006	ASP.NET Core microservices on .NET 8 (LTS) as the committed platform for every service, not one option among several	Section B.11 — one runtime and framework across all nine services keeps tooling, hiring, and cross-service code sharing consistent; LTS alignment puts the whole platform on one upgrade schedule
007	Microsoft Entra ID (OAuth 2.0 / OpenID Connect) as the identity provider, with Azure Monitor as the default observability backend	Section B.7.1, B.9, B.11 — an Azure-first choice for identity and observability, consistent with Azure DevOps/GitHub Actions (ADR-008) and Azure Container Apps as an orchestration option, while OpenTelemetry instrumentation keeps the observability backend itself swappable
008	Azure DevOps or GitHub Actions, consistently across every service, for CI/CD	Section B.10 — one pipeline tool avoids nine services each accumulating their own bespoke build/deploy conventions
009	Optimistic concurrency (RowVersion) throughout, not pessimistic row-locking	Section B.4.4 — the platform's write pattern (many short, largely-disjoint-row transactions) makes lock contention a needless cost under pessimistic locking
