010	Three-tier service taxonomy — Platform Services, Master Data Services, Business Services — with Business Services named after their SAP-equivalent module codes (FI, SD, MM, WM, MES, QM, PM, HCM, PS)	Section B.3 — matches the actual WeavoGo_Platform repository structure exactly; familiar module naming for anyone evaluating the platform against an established ERP vocabulary
011	A Backend-for-Frontend (Weavo.Bff.Web) between the YARP gateway and web clients, distinct from the gateway itself	Section B.3.4 — keeps response-shaping for a specific frontend out of every domain service; a new frontend gets a new BFF, not changes scattered across nine Business Services
012	Strict Clean Architecture (Domain/Application/Infrastructure/API) for every service, no lighter-weight alternative for smaller services, enforced by NetArchTest as part of every build	Section B.11.1–B.11.2 — the four explicit per-layer dependency rules and their NetArchTest assertions are a failing build, not a convention someone has to remember to check in review; the same rules apply identically across all twenty-four services
013	Central Package Management (Directory.Packages.props) for NuGet versions across every service	Section B.3.4 — a shared dependency version drifting between services becomes a build-time conflict, not a runtime surprise
014	Narrow, purpose-scoped polyglot persistence: SQL Server for every service by default, PostgreSQL/TimescaleDB for WeavoMES only, MongoDB for a shared Draft & Session Store only	Section B.4.8 — the default is relational; the two exceptions each have one specific, narrow reason, not a per-service judgment call across the whole platform
015	ItemMaster stays entirely on SQL Server, matching Chapters 4–13's schema and Chapter 10.9's DDL with no reconciliation needed; MongoDB is used only for a shared, cross-service Draft & Session Store, never for committed business data	Section B.4.8.1 — the working-draft/session concern is genuinely schemaless (form shape varies by form type) and genuinely not a system of record, which is a materially different case than putting a core business entity's committed data in a document store
016	A dedicated edge zone (Cloudflare WAF/DDoS, then Kubernetes Ingress NGINX) in front of the API Gateway, rather than the Gateway being the outermost layer	Section B.7.2 — attack filtering happens before traffic reaches cluster-internal networking, not after
017	Two Backend-for-Frontend services (Weavo.Bff.Web, Weavo.Bff.Mobile) rather than one BFF serving every client type	Section B.3.4, B.2 — web and mobile clients have different aggregation and payload needs; one BFF trying to serve both accumulates client-specific branching the same way a shared domain service would
018	Transactional Outbox Pattern for every published domain event, not direct publish-after-commit	Section B.5.3 — closes the gap between a committed state change and a lost event on crash; the pattern applies identically regardless of which polyglot engine (ADR-014) a service uses for its own data
B.13 Appendix Summary
This appendix turned the data model specified across Chapters 4–13 and Appendix A into a deployable system: twenty-four services across three tiers (Platform, Master Data, Business — Section B.3), cut along the same bounded contexts already established, an event-driven integration layer that makes cross-module effects like Appendix A.10.3's asset-return-on-clearance actually executable at runtime, row-level multi-tenancy that matches the Company/BusinessUnit model built since Chapter 5, and the security, scalability, and observability concerns that apply uniformly across every service regardless of which chapter's tables it owns. As with every other design decision in this document, the guiding test is unchanged: a new business type, a new tenant, or a new region of operation should be configuration and one more set of rows — in Company, BusinessUnit, or a service's own routing table — not a redesign of the architecture itself.
 
Appendix C: Common Reference Master Data
Section B.3.6 flagged three Master Data Services with no schema behind them: CountryMaster, CurrencyMaster, and CustomerMaster. This appendix specifies all three — and, for CountryMaster specifically, applies the same lesson Chapter 3 already established for items: a fixed, two-level Country → State hierarchy is exactly the mistake a rigid classification scheme makes when the real world doesn't cooperate. Bangladesh divides into Division → District → Upazila, three levels deep; Singapore has no sub-national division at all; the United States has States with Counties beneath them. A single AdministrativeDivision self-reference, sized per country rather than hard-coded to one depth, is the geographic equivalent of Chapter 4's classification hierarchy — configuration, not schema, absorbs the difference between them.
C.1 CountryMaster
C.1.1 Table: Country
Primary Key: CountryId (INT IDENTITY)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
CountryId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
CountryCode	CHAR(2)	No	—	ISO 3166-1 alpha-2 code, e.g. BD, SG, US
CountryName	VARCHAR(100)	No	—	Display name
PhoneCountryCode	VARCHAR(6)	Yes	NULL	International dialing prefix, e.g. +880
Constraints: UQ_Country_Code — unique (CountryCode).
Sample data:
Id	Code	Country Name	Phone Code
1	BD	Bangladesh	+880
2	SG	Singapore	+65
3	IN	India	+91
4	US	United States	+1
5	GB	United Kingdom	+44
6	CN	China	+86
7	DE	Germany	+49
8	JP	Japan	+81
9	AE	United Arab Emirates	+971
10	MY	Malaysia	+60
C.1.2 Table: AdministrativeDivisionType
Purpose: each country names and depths its own sub-national levels — “Division” and “District” for Bangladesh, “State” and “County” for the United States, “Province” and “Prefecture” for China. This table lets a country define however many levels it actually has, labeled the way that country actually labels them, rather than forcing every country through the same two levels named the same way.
Foreign Keys: CountryId → Country
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
AdminDivisionTypeId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
CountryId	INT	No	—	The country this level naming applies to
LevelNumber	TINYINT	No	—	1 = the country's top sub-national level, 2 = the next level down, and so on
TypeName	VARCHAR(50)	No	—	What this level is called in this country, e.g. Division, State, Province
Constraints: UQ_AdminDivisionType_CountryLevel — unique (CountryId, LevelNumber).
Business rules: a country with no rows here — Singapore, in the sample data below — simply has no sub-national division level at all; this is a valid, expected state, not a gap to fill in later.
Sample data:
Id	Country Ref.	Level	Type Name
1	Bangladesh	1	Division
2	Bangladesh	2	District
3	Bangladesh	3	Upazila
4	United States	1	State
5	United States	2	County
6	China	1	Province
7	China	2	Prefecture
8	India	1	State
9	India	2	District
Singapore (CountryId 2) deliberately has no rows here at all — a country-state has no sub-national division to name in the first place, and Section C.1.3's AdministrativeDivision table needs no rows for it either.
C.1.3 Table: AdministrativeDivision
Purpose: the actual divisions, at whatever depth a given country's AdministrativeDivisionType rows define — a self-referencing tree, the same self-reference pattern Department (Appendix A.1.1) and BOMComponent (Section 6.5.2) already use elsewhere in this document, applied here to geography instead of an org chart or a bill of materials.
Foreign Keys: CountryId → Country; AdminDivisionTypeId → AdministrativeDivisionType; ParentDivisionId → AdministrativeDivision.AdminDivisionId (nullable, self-referencing)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
AdminDivisionId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
CountryId	INT	No	—	The country this division belongs to
AdminDivisionTypeId	INT	No	—	Which level this row represents (Section C.1.2)
ParentDivisionId	INT	Yes	NULL	The next level up; NULL for a country's top-level division
DivisionCode	VARCHAR(15)	No	—	Short code, unique within its parent
DivisionName	VARCHAR(100)	No	—	Display name
Constraints: UQ_AdminDivision_ParentCode — unique (ParentDivisionId, DivisionCode); the same cycle-detection trigger Department's self-reference requires (Section 9.1) applies here too.
Business rules: depth is whatever a country's own AdministrativeDivisionType rows define — nothing in this table enforces a fixed number of levels. An address (Section C.3.1) may reference the division at whatever depth is actually known and relevant; it is never required to reference a specific level number, since not every country has one.
Sample data — Bangladesh three levels deep, the United States and China two levels, and Singapore with none at all, all coexisting in the same table with no schema difference between them:
Id	Country Ref.	Type Ref.	Parent Ref.	Code	Division Name
1	Bangladesh	Division	—	DHA	Dhaka Division
2	Bangladesh	District	1	DHA-C	Dhaka District
3	Bangladesh	Upazila	2	SAV	Savar Upazila
4	Bangladesh	Division	—	CTG	Chittagong Division
5	United States	State	—	CA	California
6	United States	County	5	LA	Los Angeles County
7	United States	State	—	NY	New York
8	China	Province	—	GD	Guangdong Province
9	China	Prefecture	8	SZ	Shenzhen Prefecture
10	India	State	—	MH	Maharashtra
C.2 CurrencyMaster
C.2.1 Table: Currency
Primary Key: CurrencyId (INT IDENTITY)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
CurrencyId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
CurrencyCode	CHAR(3)	No	—	ISO 4217 code, e.g. BDT, USD
CurrencyName	VARCHAR(50)	No	—	Display name
DecimalPlaces	TINYINT	No	2	Minor-unit precision — 2 for most currencies, 0 for currencies with no subunit in practice (e.g. Yen)
IsBaseCurrency	BIT	No	0	1 for the group's single reporting/base currency
Constraints: UQ_Currency_Code — unique (CurrencyCode); at most one row may have IsBaseCurrency = 1, enforced by a filtered unique index.
Sample data — BDT as the group's base currency, matching Section 6.2.3's original SupplierPrice examples:
Id	Code	Currency Name	Decimals	Base?
1	BDT	Bangladeshi Taka	2	Yes
2	USD	US Dollar	2	No
3	EUR	Euro	2	No
4	SGD	Singapore Dollar	2	No
5	INR	Indian Rupee	2	No
6	GBP	British Pound	2	No
7	JPY	Japanese Yen	0	No
8	CNY	Chinese Yuan	2	No
9	AED	UAE Dirham	2	No
10	MYR	Malaysian Ringgit	2	No
C.2.2 Table: ExchangeRate
Foreign Keys: CurrencyId → Currency
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
ExchangeRateId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
CurrencyId	INT	No	—	The non-base currency being converted
RateToBaseCurrency	DECIMAL(18,6)	No	—	Multiply an amount in CurrencyId by this to get the base-currency (BDT) equivalent
RateDate	DATE	No	—	Date this rate applies
Constraints: UQ_ExchangeRate_CurrencyDate — unique (CurrencyId, RateDate); CK_ExchangeRate_Positive — CHECK (RateToBaseCurrency > 0).
Business rules: converting a SupplierPrice or SalesPrice amount (Sections 6.2.3, 6.3.1) to base currency resolves against the ExchangeRate row with the latest RateDate on or before the transaction date — never a future rate, and never left unconverted. This is the mechanism Section 9.2's multi-currency purchasing exception anticipated without yet specifying; SupplierPrice.Currency and SalesPrice.Currency are amended in Section C.7 to reference Currency.CurrencyId directly rather than a bare ISO code.
Sample data (base currency: BDT):
Id	Currency Ref.	Rate to BDT	Rate Date
1	USD	119.5000	2026-07-01
2	EUR	129.8000	2026-07-01
3	SGD	88.4000	2026-07-01
4	INR	1.4300	2026-07-01
C.3 Universal Address
C.3.1 Table: Address
Purpose: one reusable address shape for every entity in the platform that needs one — Customer (Section C.4), and any future Supplier, Company, or Warehouse address — rather than each entity inventing its own address fields. City and postal/ZIP code stay free text, deliberately: city-name volume and international format variability do not warrant the same governance Country and AdministrativeDivision earn as tax and reporting jurisdictions elsewhere in this document (Section 6.4.2's TaxMapping).
Foreign Keys: CountryId → Country; AdminDivisionId → AdministrativeDivision (nullable)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
AddressId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
AddressLine1	VARCHAR(200)	No	—	Street address
AddressLine2	VARCHAR(200)	Yes	NULL	Additional address detail
City	VARCHAR(100)	No	—	Free text
AdminDivisionId	INT	Yes	NULL	The most specific division actually known — a District, a State, whatever depth applies; NULL where the country has no division level at all (Section C.1.2)
PostalCode	VARCHAR(20)	Yes	NULL	Free text — format varies too widely by country to validate centrally
CountryId	INT	No	—	Always required, independently of whether AdminDivisionId is set
Constraints: CK_Address_DivisionCountryMatch — when AdminDivisionId is set, its AdministrativeDivision.CountryId must equal this row's own CountryId, enforced at the application layer (Section 12.1) rather than a database CHECK, since it requires a join SQL Server's CHECK constraints cannot express directly.
Sample data — a three-level Bangladesh address, a two-level US address, and a Singapore address with no division level at all, in the same table with no structural difference between them:
Id	Address Line 1	City	Division Ref.	Postal Code	Country Ref.
1	House 12, Road 5	Savar	Savar Upazila	1340	Bangladesh
2	350 5th Avenue	New York	New York (State)	10118	United States
3	1 Raffles Place	Singapore	— (no division level)	048616	Singapore
4	221B Baker Street	London	—	NW1 6XE	United Kingdom
Row 3 is the point of this table: Singapore's AdminDivisionId is NULL not because the data is incomplete, but because Section C.1.2 defines no AdministrativeDivisionType for Singapore at all — there is no division to reference. Row 1 references a level-3 Upazila directly, skipping past the intermediate Division and District only in the sense that they are reachable by walking AdminDivisionId's parent chain, not because they needed to be repeated on the address itself.
C.3.2 Table: AddressFormatTemplate
Purpose: which fields an address form shows, in what order, and under what label — varying by country the same way ShiftGroup lets a rotation vary by business unit (Appendix A.7.1.2) or AttributeTemplate lets a form vary by item family (Section 4.8). Exactly one country's template is the platform default: Bangladesh, since both Companies specified in this document (Section 5.2.1's WeavoGo Group Ltd. and That's It Sports Ware Ltd.) are Bangladesh-registered — a new address form defaults to Bangladesh's layout until a different country is actually selected, rather than defaulting to no layout at all.
Foreign Keys: CountryId → Country
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
AddressFormatTemplateId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
CountryId	INT	No	—	The country this layout applies to
TemplateName	VARCHAR(100)	No	—	Display name
IsPlatformDefault	BIT	No	0	1 for exactly one template — the layout shown before a country is selected
Constraints: UQ_AddressFormatTemplate_Country — unique (CountryId); at most one row may have IsPlatformDefault = 1, enforced by a filtered unique index.
Sample data:
Id	Country Ref.	Template Name	Platform Default?
1	Bangladesh	Bangladesh Standard Address	Yes
2	United States	US Standard Address	No
3	United Kingdom	UK Standard Address	No
C.3.3 Table: AddressFormatField
Purpose: the ordered, labeled fields within a template — the same Template/TemplateItem shape already used twice in this document (TemplateAttribute, Section 4.9; EmployeeTemplateAttribute, Appendix A.3.3.3), applied a third time to address layout instead of item or employee attributes.
Foreign Keys: AddressFormatTemplateId → AddressFormatTemplate
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
AddressFormatFieldId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
AddressFormatTemplateId	INT	No	—	Parent template
FieldOrder	TINYINT	No	—	Display order, 1 first
FieldLabel	VARCHAR(50)	No	—	The label shown on the form, e.g. “Upazila / Thana”
MapsToColumn	VARCHAR(30)	No	—	Which Address (Section C.3.1) column or AdministrativeDivision level this field renders
Constraints: UQ_AddressFormatField_TemplateOrder — unique (AddressFormatTemplateId, FieldOrder).
Sample data — Bangladesh's field order and labels contrasted against the United States, both rendering the same underlying Address columns (Section C.3.1) differently:
Order	Template Ref.	Field Label	Maps To
1	Bangladesh	House / Road / Block No.	AddressLine1
2	Bangladesh	Area / Village	AddressLine2
3	Bangladesh	Upazila / Thana	City
4	Bangladesh	District	AdminDivisionId (District level)
5	Bangladesh	Division	AdminDivisionId (Division level)
6	Bangladesh	Post Code	PostalCode
1	United States	Street Address	AddressLine1
2	United States	Apt. / Suite	AddressLine2
3	United States	City	City
4	United States	State	AdminDivisionId (State level)
5	United States	ZIP Code	PostalCode
“District” and “Division” in the Bangladesh template resolve by walking AdministrativeDivision's parent chain (Section C.1.3) up from whatever level the address actually stored — they are rendering instructions, not additional columns to populate; the underlying Address row (Section C.3.1) is identical in shape regardless of which template a user saw while entering it.
C.4 CustomerMaster
C.4.1 Table: Customer
Purpose: mirrors Supplier (Section 6.2.1) almost exactly — the sales-side counterpart of the same relationship shape, closing the gap Section 6.3.1's CustomerItem left open since Chapter 6.
Primary Key: CustomerId (INT IDENTITY)
Foreign Keys: CountryId → Country
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
CustomerId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
CustomerCode	VARCHAR(15)	No	—	Unique customer code
CustomerName	VARCHAR(150)	No	—	Legal or trading name
CountryId	INT	No	—	Country of registration
TaxRegistrationNo	VARCHAR(30)	Yes	NULL	VAT/GST/tax ID
PaymentTermsDays	INT	No	30	Standard payment terms
CreditLimit	DECIMAL(18,2)	Yes	NULL	Maximum outstanding balance permitted; NULL = no enforced limit
Constraints: UQ_Customer_Code — unique (CustomerCode).
Sample data — finally giving Retail Buyer A and Retail Buyer B (Section 6.3.1's SalesPrice examples) real Customer rows:
Id	Code	Customer Name	Country Ref.	Payment Terms	Credit Limit
1	RETAIL-A	Retail Buyer A	United States	45 days	500,000.00
2	RETAIL-B	Retail Buyer B	United Kingdom	30 days	350,000.00
C.4.2 Table: CustomerAddress
Purpose: bill-to and ship-to addresses, since a customer routinely needs both and they are frequently different — a junction onto the universal Address table (Section C.3.1), not a second copy of address fields.
Foreign Keys: CustomerId → Customer; AddressId → Address
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
CustomerAddressId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
CustomerId	INT	No	—	The customer this address belongs to
AddressId	INT	No	—	The address (Section C.3.1)
AddressType	VARCHAR(10)	No	—	BillTo or ShipTo
IsDefault	BIT	No	0	The default address for its AddressType
Constraints: CK_CustomerAddress_Type — CHECK (AddressType IN ('BillTo','ShipTo')); at most one IsDefault = 1 row per (CustomerId, AddressType), enforced by a filtered unique index.
Sample data:
Id	Customer Ref.	Address Ref.	Type	Default?
1	Retail Buyer A	350 5th Avenue, New York (Id 2)	BillTo	Yes
2	Retail Buyer B	221B Baker Street, London (Id 4)	BillTo	Yes
C.5 SizeMaster
Size is exactly the kind of dimension that breaks a single fixed scale: an adult apparel size runs XS–XXL, a pair of trousers is sized by waist inches, a shoe is numbered completely differently depending on whether the label says US, UK, or EU — the same physical foot gets three different numbers. SizeMaster is built the same way UOM/UOMConversion (Sections 5.4–5.5) was built for units of measure: a named group per sizing system, an ordered set of values within it, and an explicit conversion table for the cases where two systems describe the same physical size differently.
C.5.1 Table: SizeGroup
Primary Key: SizeGroupId (INT IDENTITY)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
SizeGroupId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
GroupCode	VARCHAR(20)	No	—	Unique code, e.g. ADULT-ALPHA
GroupName	VARCHAR(100)	No	—	Display name
SizeCategory	VARCHAR(30)	No	—	A free-text label for filtering and grouping only — Apparel, Footwear, ICT, Construction, Medical, Packaging, or any label a deployment finds useful
Constraints: UQ_SizeGroup_Code — unique (GroupCode). SizeCategory deliberately carries no CHECK constraint — an earlier revision of this table restricted it to ('Apparel','Footwear','Universal'), which quietly assumed sizing only ever applies to clothes and shoes. WeavoGo spans garments, ICT, construction, tea estate, hospital, and logistics (Section 3.2's reference dataset); a size system is as likely to describe a steel rod's diameter or a laptop's screen as a shirt's collar, and nothing in this table should have to be amended to add the next industry.
Sample data — six industries in the same table, none requiring a schema change to add:
Id	Code	Group Name	Category
1	ADULT-ALPHA	Adult Apparel (Alpha)	Apparel
2	MENS-WAIST	Men's Bottoms (Waist, Inches)	Apparel
3	WOMENS-DRESS-US	Women's Dress (US Numeric)	Apparel
4	KIDS-AGE	Kids Apparel (Age-Based)	Apparel
5	BRA-US	Bra / Lingerie (Band + Cup, US)	Apparel
6	FOOTWEAR-US-MEN	Footwear (US Men's)	Footwear
7	FOOTWEAR-US-WOMEN	Footwear (US Women's)	Footwear
8	FOOTWEAR-UK	Footwear (UK)	Footwear
9	FOOTWEAR-EU	Footwear (EU)	Footwear
10	ONE-SIZE	One Size / Free Size	Universal
11	ROD-DIA-MM	Steel Rod Diameter (mm)	Construction
12	CEMENT-BAG-KG	Cement Bag Weight (kg)	Construction
13	LAPTOP-SCREEN-IN	Laptop Screen Size (Inches)	ICT
14	MED-GLOVE	Medical Glove Size	Medical
15	CONTAINER-FT	Shipping Container Size (Feet)	Logistics
C.5.2 Table: Size
Purpose: the ordered values within a group — SortOrder is what lets a UI render sizes S, M, L in size order rather than alphabetical or insertion order.
Foreign Keys: SizeGroupId → SizeGroup
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
SizeId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
SizeGroupId	INT	No	—	Parent group
SizeCode	VARCHAR(10)	No	—	The value itself, e.g. M, 32, 9, 16mm
SizeLabel	VARCHAR(30)	Yes	NULL	Optional longer display form, e.g. “Medium” for SizeCode “M”
SortOrder	INT	No	—	Ascending size progression within the group
Constraints: UQ_Size_GroupCode — unique (SizeGroupId, SizeCode); UQ_Size_GroupSort — unique (SizeGroupId, SortOrder).
Sample data — an apparel group and a footwear group as before, plus the three new non-apparel groups, with Rod Diameter and Cement Bag Weight deliberately populated with the exact values two items already specified in this document already carry (CON-STL-000014's 16 mm Diameter attribute, Section 3.x's classification example; CON-CEM-000013's 50 KG cement bag), so the same SizeMaster schema resolves against real, previously-established data rather than newly-invented examples:
Group Ref.	Size Code	Size Label	Sort Order
Adult Apparel (Alpha)	S	Small	3
Adult Apparel (Alpha)	M	Medium	4
Adult Apparel (Alpha)	L	Large	5
Footwear (US Men's)	9	—	3
One Size / Free Size	OS	One Size	1
Steel Rod Diameter (mm)	8	—	1
Steel Rod Diameter (mm)	10	—	2
Steel Rod Diameter (mm)	12	—	3
Steel Rod Diameter (mm)	16	—	4
Steel Rod Diameter (mm)	20	—	5
Steel Rod Diameter (mm)	25	—	6
Cement Bag Weight (kg)	25	—	1
Cement Bag Weight (kg)	50	—	2
Laptop Screen Size (Inches)	13	—	1
Laptop Screen Size (Inches)	14	—	2
Laptop Screen Size (Inches)	15.6	—	3
Laptop Screen Size (Inches)	16	—	4
MS Rod Grade 60 16 mm (CON-STL-000014) and Dell Latitude 7450 Laptop (ItemId 6, first introduced in Section 3.8) both resolve their sizing through this same table — the Steel Rod's existing Diameter attribute value of “16 mm” matches Size row “16” in Steel Rod Diameter (mm) exactly, and a SCREEN_SIZE attribute added to the Dell Latitude's AttributeTemplate (Section 4.8's Laptop template) would resolve against Laptop Screen Size (Inches) the identical way ADULT-ALPHA resolves for a T-shirt's SIZE attribute — one AttributeDefinition pattern (Section 4.7), one SizeMaster, three industries.
C.5.3 Table: SizeConversion
Purpose: the same physical size, expressed in two different groups — the mechanism a shoe listed as “US 9” needs to also show “UK 8 / EU 42” without three separate ItemMaster rows for the identical shoe.
Foreign Keys: FromSizeId → Size; ToSizeId → Size
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
SizeConversionId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
FromSizeId	INT	No	—	Size in the source group
ToSizeId	INT	No	—	The equivalent size in the target group
Constraints: UQ_SizeConversion_Pair — unique (FromSizeId, ToSizeId).
Business rules: conversion is defined pairwise and is not assumed to be transitively derivable in the schema — US→UK and US→EU are each their own rows, not computed from one another, since real-world shoe size conversions are not perfectly linear across brands.
Sample data — US Men's Footwear sizes 7–11 mapped to their UK and EU equivalents:
Id	US Men's	UK	EU
1	7	6	40
2	8	7	41
3	9	8	42
4	10	9	43
5	11	10	44
Size is captured on an item the same way any other variable characteristic is: as an ItemAttribute value (Section 4.11) against an AttributeDefinition for SIZE, with the stored value validated against Size.SizeCode for the item's assigned SizeGroup rather than left as an unchecked string — the same Application-layer validation discipline Section 12.1's matrix already applies everywhere else. Formally linking a specific Item, Size, and Color into one sellable, stockable SKU-level variant is not yet specified in this document and would be a natural extension of Chapter 4's classification hierarchy, not a change to it.
C.6 ColorMaster
The same grouping-plus-value shape as SizeMaster, one level simpler since colors don't need a cross-system conversion table — a color family groups related shades for filtering and reporting (“show all Blues”), and each color carries a hex code for consistent digital display across the web micro-frontends and mobile app (Section B.2). Nothing about the shape below is apparel-specific: the same table names a T-shirt's fabric color, a laptop's chassis finish, or a delivery van's paint code with no structural difference between them — Color's sample data closes with exactly that proof.
C.6.1 Table: ColorFamily
Primary Key: ColorFamilyId (INT IDENTITY)
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
ColorFamilyId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
FamilyCode	VARCHAR(20)	No	—	Unique code, e.g. RED, BLUE
FamilyName	VARCHAR(50)	No	—	Display name
Constraints: UQ_ColorFamily_Code — unique (FamilyCode).
Sample data:
Id	Code	Family Name
1	NEUTRAL	Neutrals
2	RED	Reds
3	BLUE	Blues
4	GREEN	Greens
5	YELLOW	Yellows
6	ORANGE	Oranges
7	PURPLE	Purples
8	PINK	Pinks
9	BROWN	Browns
10	METALLIC	Metallics
11	MULTI	Multi-Color / Print
C.6.2 Table: Color
Foreign Keys: ColorFamilyId → ColorFamily
Column	Data Type	Null	Default	Description
ColorId	INT IDENTITY	No	auto	Surrogate primary key
ColorFamilyId	INT	No	—	Parent family
ColorCode	VARCHAR(20)	No	—	Unique code, e.g. NAVY, MAROON
ColorName	VARCHAR(50)	No	—	Display name
HexCode	CHAR(7)	Yes	NULL	#RRGGBB display value; NULL for Multi-Color/Print, which has no single hex value
PantoneCode	VARCHAR(20)	Yes	NULL	Textile industry standard reference, where the group maintains one
Constraints: UQ_Color_Code — unique (ColorCode).
Sample data — the colors in common use across the apparel catalogue, spanning every family:
Id	Family Ref.	Code	Color Name	Hex	Pantone
1	Neutrals	BLACK	Black	#000000	—
2	Neutrals	WHITE	White	#FFFFFF	—
3	Neutrals	CHARCOAL	Charcoal Grey	#36454F	—
4	Neutrals	LTGREY	Light Grey	#D3D3D3	—
5	Neutrals	BEIGE	Beige	#F5F5DC	—
6	Neutrals	KHAKI	Khaki	#C3B091	—
7	Blues	NAVY	Navy	#000080	19-4052 TPX
8	Blues	ROYAL	Royal Blue	#4169E1	—
9	Blues	SKYBLUE	Sky Blue	#87CEEB	—
10	Blues	TURQ	Turquoise	#40E0D0	—
11	Reds	RED	Red	#FF0000	18-1664 TPX
12	Reds	MAROON	Maroon	#800000	—
13	Reds	BURGUNDY	Burgundy	#800020	—
14	Greens	FORESTGRN	Forest Green	#228B22	—
15	Greens	OLIVE	Olive	#808000	—
16	Yellows	YELLOW	Yellow	#FFFF00	—
17	Yellows	MUSTARD	Mustard	#FFDB58	—
18	Oranges	ORANGE	Orange	#FFA500	—
19	Purples	PURPLE	Purple	#800080	—
20	Purples	LAVENDER	Lavender	#E6E6FA	—
21	Pinks	HOTPINK	Hot Pink	#FF69B4	—
22	Pinks	BABYPINK	Baby Pink	#F4C2C2	—

24	Browns	TAN	Tan	#D2B48C	—
25	Metallics	GOLD	Gold	#FFD700	—
26	Metallics	SILVER	Silver	#C0C0C0	—
27	Multi-Color / Print	MULTI	Multi-Color / Print	—	—
28	Neutrals	SPACEGREY	Space Grey	#535150	—
Row 28, Space Grey, is the cross-industry proof: it is the exact chassis color a real Dell Latitude carries, sitting in the Neutrals family alongside Charcoal and Light Grey with no separate “ICT colors” table required. Row 27, Multi-Color/Print, is deliberate in the opposite direction: a printed or patterned fabric genuinely has no single representative hex value, and forcing one would misrepresent the item rather than describe it — HexCode stays NULL for exactly this row, the same way AttributeDefinition (Section 4.7) allows a NULL UnitOfMeasure for attributes where a unit doesn't apply.
C.7 Amendments to Earlier Chapters
Five fields, in four earlier chapters, left as free text or an undefined reference specifically because no governed master existed yet — all five now resolve against the tables in this appendix:
•	Company.Country (Section 5.2.1) was VARCHAR(60); it is now CountryId, an INT FOREIGN KEY to Country.CountryId.
•	Supplier.Country (Section 6.2.1) was VARCHAR(60); it is now CountryId, an INT FOREIGN KEY to Country.CountryId.
•	Person.Nationality (Appendix A.3.1) was VARCHAR(60); it is now CountryId, an INT FOREIGN KEY to Country.CountryId — Michael Tan's Nationality (Section A.3.1's sample data) resolves to Country row 2 (Singapore).
•	SupplierPrice.Currency and SalesPrice.Currency (Sections 6.2.3, 6.3.1) were CHAR(3) ISO codes; both are now CurrencyId, an INT FOREIGN KEY to Currency.CurrencyId, resolved to base currency through ExchangeRate (Section C.2.2) exactly as Section 9.2 anticipated.
•	CustomerItem.CustomerId (Section 6.3.1) was described only as “FK to Customer master (not detailed in this document)”; it is now a real, enforced FOREIGN KEY to Customer.CustomerId (Section C.4.1).
•	AttributeDefinition rows for SIZE and COLOR (Section 4.7), wherever any item family's attribute template includes them — not only garments — now validate their ItemAttribute.ValueText against Size.SizeCode (Section C.5.2) or Color.ColorCode (Section C.6.2) at the Application layer, rather than accepting an unchecked string. A Steel Rod's Diameter and a Laptop's screen size resolve through the identical mechanism as a T-shirt's Size, each simply pointed at its own SizeGroup — the same validation discipline Section 12.1's matrix already applies to every other attribute, now proven across three industries rather than assumed to work for only one.
None of these amendments change any sample data value already stated elsewhere in this document — “Bangladesh,” “USD,” and “Retail Buyer A” all still mean exactly what they meant when first introduced. What changes is that those values are now enforced, governed references instead of strings a typo could silently diverge from.
C.8 Chapter Summary
Fifteen tables close every gap this appendix set out to close, and each one uses a pattern this document had already proven somewhere else: AdministrativeDivision's self-referencing, per-country-configurable depth is Chapter 3's classification lesson applied to geography; AddressFormatTemplate/AddressFormatField is the Template/TemplateItem shape from Section 4.9 applied to address layout; SizeGroup/Size/SizeConversion is Section 5.4–5.5's UOM/UOMConversion shape applied to sizing systems; ColorFamily/Color is the same grouping idea one level simpler. Bangladesh, the United States, China, and Singapore all coexist in the same geography tables with no schema difference between them; a shoe sized US, UK, or EU coexists in the same size tables the same way; and a T-shirt's Size, a Steel Rod's Diameter, and a Laptop's screen size all coexist in that same SizeGroup/Size pair with no per-industry table required, exactly as ColorFamily/Color names a fabric's shade and a laptop's chassis finish identically. Section B.3.2's Master Data Services table and Section B.3.6's coverage roadmap are updated accordingly.
